 for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy.
                                    ABSTRACT




The use of the estímation-before-modellíng (EBM) two step identication
procedure for the detennination of aircra aerodynamic derivatives from ight
test data is analysed and illustrated.  In the rst step of the identication
procedure the usual Extended Kalman Filter (EKF ) associated with the Moded
Bryson-Frazíer (MBF) smoother is compared with a new altemative ltering and
smoothing process. The new smoother is simpler and less computationally
demanding than the MBF smoother. However, its main advantage is that it
enables simultaneous data smoothing with state derivative estimation,
                                                                      thereby
avoiding the need for a separate differentiation algorithm. The new smoother-
differentiator has an important feature that is the detennination of the noise
characteristics of the measurement signal under analysis prior to the
                                                                     smoothing
process.  This is done by variance matching between the theoretical and
measured autocorrelation of the innovation process generated by a Kalman lter.
The new technique is compared with the old one by
                                                     detennining the aerodynamic
models for a EMB-312 Tucano dutch roll manoeuvre. It is demonstrated that
the new smoother may be used to replace the MBF. Otherwise the new
                                                                          technique
is used in the analysis of the Handley Page Jetstream-100
                                                                 aircra lowšs-peed
controls free phugoid trying to identify the contribution of the
                                                                    power Variation
observed during the phugoid to the stability of the oscillation.
                                                                 Finally the models
obtained from the phugoid analysis are reprocessed using the Total Least
                                                                             Square
regression and the results are compared with those om the ordinary Least
Square formulation.

              INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION



1.1 Introduction


I the last three decades, system identication was
                                                    developed as a powerful
'mathematical technique for determining the properties of systems from the
measurement and analysis of their relevant input and output parameters. I
particular, the application of system identication to the detenrination of aircraft
stability and control derivatives 'om ight data has been extensively developed.
I this area previous approaches were based mainly on time
                                                             consuming steady-
state measurements, on the measurement of free oscillations and later, with the
advent of analog computer, by using analog-matching.


The identication of aircra parameters using modem
                                                          system identication
techniques encouraged the development of new procedures and methods of ight
testing and data analysis. This new ight test tool was used initially in military
programmes. Today, however, it is used also in civil aviation programmes as a
cost effective tool mainly to reduce development and certication time. It is used
also to identify aircra characteristics, which are not identiable
                                                                  by static ight
test techniques, such as those of aircra equipped with Automatic
                                                                   Flight Control
System.     Today's methodology allows to evaluate 'om one test run the
aerodynamic model structure and to estimate nearly all involved parameters.

The methods most commonly encountered in the literature are the
                                                                      Equatíon
Error, the Output Error, the Extended Kalman Fílter, the Frequency Domain
Approach and the Maximum Likelíhood Method. One of the rst analytical
methods used in aircraft parameter estimation was the
                                                         equation error method
which is based on the linear regression technique. Under this
                                                                  approach the
mathematical representation of the aircraft dynamics or
                                                        aerodynamics is written
as,


                           y(t)=®0 +®1x1+~-~+®_1x_l                    (1.1)

The dependent variable y(t) may represent
                                            any one of the aircra states or their
derivatives, forming then a linear expression i the unknowns 6),.. The
independent variables are the aircra states in any combination, linear or non-
linear. The unknown terms ®, are obtained
                                           by the least square fitting approach.



                                        l
With the advent of stochastic methods and the development of powerful digital
computers new solutions to the aircraft parameter estimation problem have been
formulated and developed. The equation error method has survived and has been
enhanced by the addition of state estimation techniques. Today, regression
analysis has become a important tool for aircraft aerodynamic and control
modelling especially for non-linear ight conditions such as stall, spin, etc. In
particular, when associated with the Stepwise Regression Procedure it became an
important tool in aerodynamic modelling because its merges the simplicity of the
linear regression with the statistical possibility to analyse how the
                                                                       independent
variables contribute to the overall t of the model.          In addition, it allows
identication among the variables not yet included in the model the variable
which best correlates with the present model, identifying in this
                                                                  way a candidate
variable to be included in the next iteration.


The objective of this research is an analysis of the use of the Stepwise
                                                                      Regression
procedure for the determination of aircrafc aerodynamic and control parameters in
a two step process, the so called
                                  Estimation-Before-Modelling (EBM) method. In
the rst step an Extended Kalman Filter and a smoother are used for state
estimation. I the second step the linear regression, in the fonn of a
                                                                        stepwise
regression procedure, is used for aerodynamic modelling using the data generated
in the rst step as input. The main topic of research is the
                                                            comparative analysis
of two different ltering and smoothing algorithm
                                                 congurations, as follows;

(i)    A standard Extended Kalman Filter and a Modied Bryson-Frazier (MBF)
       smoother.


(ii)   An Iterated Extended    Kalman    Filter and    a Fixed Lag     smoother-
       differentiator.


The rst conguration is the most frequently identied in the literature. The
second conguration presents a more sophisticated lter and a
                                                            simplied xed
lag smoother algorithm. The new smoother algorithn is less computationally
demandíng than the Modied Bryson-Frazier smoother and its main advantage is
that it enables simultaneous data smoothing and differentiation. It is an
                                                                           important
feature because in aircra aerodynamic structure
                                                            modelling some state
derivatives are independent variables in the model. This avoids the use of a
separate differentiation algorithm or to calculate the derivatives directly from the
state equations which in general produces noisy derivatives. Otherwise the
calculated derivatives are very clean and representative of the actual derivatives
because the noise characteristics of the data signal under
                                                        analysis is determinated
prior to the smoothing process by variance matching between the theoretical and
the measured autoconelation of the innovation
                                               process generated by a Kalman




                                         2


                                                                                       l
lter. The comparative analysis between the two algorithms is perfonned
                                                                       by
analysing the dutch roll manoeuvre of a EMB-312 Tucano Trainer.

The research also illustrates the reconstructibility analysis of the state estimation
process and the identiability of the parameter estimation problem, for linear
models, and by analogy extends its validity for the non-linear model used in the
research.


I addition, attempts are made to evaluate the propulsive                         ¬
                                                           efciency, the aircraï
parasitic drag and the induced drag from the analysis of the transient response in
airspeed for small power changes in level ight by using the scheme proposed by
Bull and Bridges [24]. I the analysis the dynamic and obsevation models are
reforrnulated and a Extended Kalman Filter is used instead of the Maximum
Likelihood proposed in the original scheme.


F inally the new estimation process is applied to a
                                                     Handley Page (HP) Jetstream-
 100 aircraft phugoid analysis which has its stability closely related to the
                                                                              engine
power control system which varies the engine power as the aircra airspeed
changes naturally during the manoeuvre. Final aerodynamic models are then
reprocessed by using the regression process fonnulated as Total Least Square and
the results are compared against the traditional Least
                                                                 Square regression
technique.




1.2 Background


From the early years of aviation, many methods were
                                                         developed and used to
estimate aircra aerodynamic characteristics. Therefore, reliable
                                                                 methodology for
determining aircraft ying and handling characteristics was developed late in the
sixties with the development of system identication and its
                                                               application to the
spacial programmes.

The   methods    most cormnonly encountered in modem aircra
                                                                    parameter
estimation literature are the Equatíon Error, the
                                                  Output Error, the Extended
Kalman Filter, the Frequency Domain Approach and the
                                                        generalised Maximum
Likelihood Method.


The simplest analytical technique for aircra
                                                 parameter estimation is the
Equatíon Error Method that is based on the principle of Least Square tting. It
represents the application of linear regression to each equation of motion
separately. It gives biased estimation due to the measurements noise in the state




                                         3
and input variables and requires the measurement of all state variables involved
in the process.


The Output Error Method minimizes the error between the output of a model and
the measured aircraft response by iterative model parameter
                                                               adjustment. The
process assumes that only the measured output is cormpted by noise and there are
no other disturbances on the aircraft. The optimization is non-linear and the
modied Newton-Raphson technique is usually applied in a iterative fashion.
The output error method is also called Maximum Likelihood method when this
criterion is used in the cost function. It is susceptible to
                                                             degradation when
process noise exists in the data.

The Extended Kalman Filter (EKF) is an approximate stochastic lter for non-
linear systems based on rst-order linearisation. It is only a rst-order minimum
variance lter.    It estimates simultaneously the aircra state variables and the
other unknown parameters (the aerodynamic derivatives).    This is done by an
indirect approach where the parameter estimation problem is transfonned into a
state estimation problem by aticially dening the unknown
                                                               system parameters
as additional state variables, leading to a non-linear
                                                      augmented system even for
systems that were originally linear. Once this is done the ordinary Kalman lter
equations can be applied for the state estimation of the new augmented state
vector. Its main disadvantage is that it requires a
                                                        priori knowledge of the
covariances which are normally unknown.          If the a priori values for the
parameters are poor, the method exhibits poor convergence.


Frequency Domain methods may be fonnulated for aircraft parameter estimation
using existing time domain methods such as equation error and output error. For
this fonnulation it is necessary to transform, by Fourier
                                                          integral, the model and
the data and formulate a cost function. The least
                                                    square criterion may be used
and its transfonnation to the frequency domain in obtained
                                                            by applying Parceval's
theorem. The cost function may be then minimized in the least
                                                                     square sense.
Analogously a cost function may be fonnulated for the output error method and
its minimization may be perfonned iteratively
                                                 by using the modied Newton-
Raphson algorithm. The main disadvantage of these techniques is the processing
time which is up to 50% more than the time
                                             required for an equivalent time
domain calculation.


The  generalized Maximum Likelihood estimation method consists of a
combination of the Kalman Filter (or EKF
                                         depending on linear or non-linear
system, respectively) for estimating the state and a modied Newton-Raphson
iterative procedure for estimating parameters. In general the unknown
                                                                       parameters
can include stability and control derivatives, bias tenns in the state and
                                                                           output



                                        4
equations, initial conditions for state variables, measurement and process noise
covariances. When it is assumed that no process noise exists, the method
reduces to the output error method and the estimates are obtained by integration
of the equations of motion only. It has become the most used, most powerful and
possibly the most accurate technique in use today.


Irrespective of the method used, it is necessary to fonnulate a mathematical model
for the aircraft under test. The problem of modelling the aircra
                                                                      aerodynamic
characteristics raises, therefore, the question of how complex the model should
be. A more complex model can be justified for the correct
                                                            description of aircraft
motion. However, in the case of parameter estimation, it is not clear what should
be the best relationship between model complexity, measurement information and
quality of the results. In general, linear models can produce significant results.
Therefore, the development of modern aircra highly dependent on complex
digital ight control system with dynamics described by high order models, as
well as the interest in poststall and spin, has created the need to extend
                                                                           parameter
estimation to ight regimes where non-linear aerodynamic effects could be
significant.

The stepwise regression (SR) was introduced to aircra
                                                           parameter estimation as
an efcient altemative for the determination of
                                                    aerodynamic model structure
from ight data. It is seen as a good altemative to be used where non-linear terms
are required in the analysis because non-linear combinations of state variables as
independent variables in the regression are still linear for the regression process.
Othervvise it allows the introduction and removal of independent variables from
the regression model by simple analysis of the correlation coefcients calculated
at each iteration.


The use of the stepwise regression procedure was
                                                    proposed in 1967 by Gerlach
[1] in the Netherlands for ight test instrumentation calibration to be used in non-
steady tests. The first use of the stewise regression in aerodynamic modelling
was presented in the U.S.A in 1974 by Hall, Gupta and
                                                            Tyler [2] when the
method was used as a start-up procedure of aerodynamic
                                                           modelling to be used
together with a Maximum Likelihood Estimator. A second work in this area was
presented in 1975 by V. Klein [3] in the Craneld College of Aeronautícs when
the stepwise regression procedure was used for aircraft
                                                          aerodynamic structure
modelling. The method was used again in 1979 by The Technical University of
Del and the National Aerospace Laboratory
                                              (NLR) [4] for aerodynamic model
identification. Further development of the stepwise regression method has been
carried out in the U.S.A by Klein, Batterson and
                                                  Murphy [5]. I this work the
aircraft equations of motion have been presented in
                                                            general form with
aerodynamic force and moment coefcients expressed in terms of multivariable




                                         5
polynomials in input and output variables. The stepwise regression has been
modied by the addition of a constraint where the regression starts with the
introduction of linear terms only to the model. After all linear tems have been
included then non-linear terms are analysed. This was called the Modified
Stepwise Regression - (MSR). Later Klein et al [6] used the stepwise regression
procedure associated with the evaluation of data partitioning analysis, i.e., the
determination of aerodynamic models valid for a specic condition, for
                                                                        example,
applicable for a narrow range of incidence angle. More recently, Hess and Ly [7]
proposed the use of the stepwise regression for the detennination of model
structure for aircrafr simulation while Stengel and Sri-Jayantha
                                                                  [8] used it for
aircra stall and spin tests. At Craneld University new research has been carried
out by Hinds [9] when the MSR procedure was used to estimate the
                                                                     stability and
control parameters of a small B.Ae. Hawk aircra model own in a
                                                                        dynamic
wind tunnel facility. The most recent applications of the
                                                            stepwise regression
procedure were due to Haas and Imber [lO], also in 1994, and dealt with the
identication of helicopter component loads and by Laban [11] also in 1994
when the MSR procedure was used for on-line identication of aircraï              ¬
aerodynamic model structure with the linear regression fonnulated as Total Least
Square.

Since the regression procedure requires the knowledge of the aircra states and
aircra states can be measured or estimated
                                             independently of the aerodynamic
model the whole identication procedure has been transformed into a two
                                                                          step
process. The rst step consisting of the accurate detennination of the aircraï¬
states by using an stochastic approach.    The second step, or the parameter
estimation step, where the linear regression process is used to determine the
coefcients of the model with the data generated in the rst
                                                                   step as input. The
initial idea of separating the state and parameter estimation
                                                                problem was probably
due to Gerlach [12] in the Netherlands in 1964, when he
                                                                     pointed out that
redundant measurements could be used to con'ect
                                                      Velocity, incidence, etc. The
use of this technique has been pioneered by Hosman et all
                                                               [13] (Delft 1971) and
Wingrove [14] (Nasa 1972) when pitch angle and ight path angle were
calculated by integrating (in detenninistic fashion) rate
                                                             gyros and acceleration
measurements for the analysis of aircraft dynamic manoeuvres. With the advent
of Kalman ltering techniques the state prediction
                                                           problem becomes easily
soluble and Ulrich and Rynaski [15] (Calspan
                                                  1974), Jonkers [16] (Del 1976)
and Klein [17] (Nasa 1977) were among the rst to
                                                        investigate its use. The rst
step process became known as ight path reconstructíon technique (Delï               ¬
denomination) or compatib'l`ty check (Nasa denomination) while the state
estimation plus aerodynamic modelling became known as two
                                                          step process or
Estimation-Before-Modelling technique.     Among the rst users             of these
techniques are Mulder et al [3] (Del 1979) and Stalford [18] (1982).




                                         6
The present research programme is concemed with the use of a MSR procedure
to estimate aircra stability and control parameters from ight test data using a
two step process, state estimation-aerodynamic modelling.        I the rst step an
Extended    Kalman Filter and      a   smoother are   used    for state estimation.
Aerodynamic modelling is accomplished in the second step by using the stepwise
regression with the data estimated in the rst step. Two different congurations
of the EKF and smoother algorithns are analysed and a simplied smoother-
differentiator conguration is suggested to replace the more traditional smoother,
the Modied Bryson-Frazier.



1.3 Problem Formulation


Regarding only mathematical aspects, when system identication is applied to an
aircraft as a dynamic system, the equations of motion must be dened and the
input and output variables must be measured. The basic equations for the motion
analysis of a rigid-body are the linear momentum and angular momentum
equations, which in rotating body-axis system are given by;


                              i(mV)+ wçm/)=:ºf(®)

                              f                                        (1.2)
                              E(H)+ wr = mo)

The vectors . and 77 of equations 1.2 are, respectively, the extemal force and
the extemal moment due to aerodynamic effects, gravitational forces and
                                                                               engines
thrust. H is the angular momentum, m is the aircra mass, V is the
                                                                   Velocity and a
is the angular Velocity.   The vector ® contains the parameters that represent the
aircra aerodynamic and control characteristics and is a linear or a non-linear
function of the aircrafc states. The equations 1.2 are further
                                                               complemented by a
set of kinematic equations relating the Euler angles and the attitude rates
                                                                            forming
the system dynamic model.


The aircraft identication problem can be dened as the
                                                           detennination, from the
input and output measurements, of the structure of 5 and 777. In actual applications
3 and 77 are approximated by Taylor series expansion, i.e., the model structure is
dened, and the identication problem is reduced to a parameter estimation
problem.

A general approach of system identication applied to aircra is shown in
                                                                   Figure
1.1 where the various steps of the procedure are
                                                 presented. Below a brief



                                         7
description of each step is given reecting the research methodology used in this
work.

          1.1. Aircra Aerodynamic Model Structure Detennination.


Experimental planning includes the denition of what aircra' characteristics are
being investigated and which identication procedure is going to be used. It
includes also the selection of the instrumentation, the definition of
                                                                            ight
manoeuvres or control inputs more compatible with the identication
                                                                       procedure
and the test execution.         Regarding instrumentation, the use of system
identication methodology associated with non-steady ight manoeuvres
                                                                           places
special demands on instrumentation accuracy and resolution. Klein [19] states
that an accuracy of 0.l% (of full scale reading) is
                                                    required for the measurement
of angular accelerations, angular rates and control surfaces
                                                             deections, 0.02% is
required for airspeed and incidence angle while the remaining should be
measured with 0.5 to 1% of accuracy. I tenns of resolution at least 14 bit
                                                                           binary
is required for a digital system which means 0.006% of resolution while
                                                                        sampling
rates between 20 and 100 samples/sec are usually
                                                  required.

State Estimation or Flight path reconstructíon/Data
                                                      compatibílity check is the
next step of the identication problem. In this
                                               step the data is analysed in order



                                              8
to identify whether the manoeuvre the objectives or not and whether or not it
contains gross imperfections (spikes, drop-outs, correct sign, noise level, etc.).
Finally, state estimation is performed in order to identify unmeasured states or
those not accurately known, detennine offsets, scale factors, etc. I general the
state estimation process is perfonred independently of the definition of the
aerodynamic model, using only inertial and gravitational equations plus Euler
angles and body rate relationship (E-B-M methodology).

Model structure and parameter estimation. The denition of the model structure
and the parameter estimation technique fonr the main core of the identication
procedure. Given the aerodynamic model and the corrected ight data, the
parameter estimation procedure calculates the coefcients of the proposed model
for the available data following a optimization criteria. For the present research
the chosen parameter estimation procedure is the equation error which is based
on a linear regression teclmique. The unknown coefcients of the
                                                                aerodynamic
model are estimated by minimizing the sum of squared differences between
measured and predicted aerodynamic forces and moments which is
                                                                 represented
by the cost function;

                              J                                             (1.3)


where y() represent the measured and          jº(',®) the estimated values of the
dependent variable being 3/(',®) a linear function of the unknown aerodynamic
parameters (9.

Model validatíon is the last step of the identication process.         The resulting
model must have physical meaning, acceptable accuracy and
                                                                 good prediction
capability. Results must be compared with information available from any source,
wind-tunnel, previous measurements using different manoeuvre and/or different
estimation methods.     Prediction capabilities may be evaluated by using the
recorded input of other manoeuvre and verifying how the model
                                                                  reproduces the
aircra response.


The whole estimation process described above will be
                                                     gradually presented in the
following chapters. Chapter 2 presents mathematical models to represent aircraï
                                                                              ¬
dynamics, measurements and aerodynamic models while Chapter 3 presents the
state estimation and smoothers algorithms and
                                                analyses the problem of state
reconstructibility. Chapter 4 deals with the least square regression algorithm, the
stepwise regression procedure, identiability, collinearity and the new approach
in least square estimation, i.e., the total least square.
                                                          Chapter 5 deals with inputs
to the aircra control system. Chapter 6 presents the research results and
                                                                             Chapter
7 the conclusions.

                                     Chapter 2

                           MATHEMATICAL MODELS



2.1 Reference Frames and Transformations


The analysis of a ight dynamics problem requires in general the denitions of a
number of reference frames that are used for specifying relative
                                                                positions, relative
velocities, vector components (forces, accelerations, etc.), moments and products
of inertia and aerodynamic derivative denitions. Aircraft
                                                              equations of motion
may be written considering the observer xed to any reference frame. The choice
of a specic reference frame is a matter of preference or convenience because the
right choice may simplify the analysis process by requiring simple mathematical
treatment.
A problem may involve more than one frame of reference and so it is
                                                                          required to
fomiulate not only the reference frames but also the transfonnations
                                                                         required to
change quantities from one to other ame. I ight dynamics the motion of
interest is that of the aircra relative to the atmosphere
                                                          being required to dene at
least a body xed axes system and an earth xed axes system to relate the motion
of the former system.



2.1.1 Reference Frames


(i) Datum Reference Frame

Datum Reference Frame is a system of coordinate axes,
                                                            body xed, orthogonal
and left-handed which is used to dene the location of characteristic
                                                                      points in the
aircra. It is commonly dened by the airframe manufacturer to
                                                                  specify positions
or stations in the aircrañ and is used to dene the aircra centre of
                                                                    gravity, origin
of many other reference frames. The origin of the datum frame is
                                                                         frequently
taken at the aircra nose or even a point in the front of the nose. The X
                                                                           D axis is
in the aircraft plane of symmetry and points to its tail.         The YD axis is
perpendicular to the plane of symmety and points to the port wing while the ZD
axis is upward. Appendix B shows the datum reference frame for the Jetstream-
100 aircraft.


(ii) Inertial Reference Frame

The Inertial Reference Frame is a system of coordinate
                                                            axes, orthogonal, xed, or
in unifonn rectilinear translation relative to distant stars.




                                         lO
(iii) Earth-Fixed Reference Frame

In the analysis of dynamics of conventional aircra, the Earth rotation relative to   I
an inertial 'ame can be neglected and any frame xed to the Earth can be used as
an inertial frame. For the present analysis the Earth-Fixed Frame is dened
                                                                           by a
system of axes 0eXeYeZe where the plane OeXe , OeYe             denes the local
horizontal plane tangential to the earth surface and,


                  OeXe points to the North
                  0eYe points to theEast
                  OeZe points vertically along the' gravity vector (downwards).



(iv) Body Axes


Body axes are a orthogonal set of axes xed to the aircraft with the origin at the
aircra centre of gravity (cg) and are such that the plane OXZ is in the aircraï ¬
centre of symmetry with OZ pointing downwards, OY directed to starboard and
OX pointing to the aircraft nose.  When the aircraft is disturbed the axes move
with the air'ame and the parameters describing the
                                                     perturbance are considered
with respect to its initial undisturbed values. The table below
                                                                    presents the
perturbation parameters which are of fundamental importance in ight dynamics
analysis,


                            Perturbation Parameters


                       Drag force - component of aerodynamic force
                       Side force - component of aerodynamic force
                       Li force     - component of aerodynamic force.
                       Rolling moment
                       Pitching moment
                       Yawing moment
                       Rate of roll
                       Rate of pitch
                       Rate of yaw
                       Velocity perturbation - x axis
                       Velocity Perturbation - y axis
                       Velocity perturbation - z axis.




                                        11
(V) Wind Axes.

Wind axes describe an orthogonal reference frame whose origin is xed to the
aircraft centre of gravity and whose x-axis is directed along the resultant total
Velocity vector V of the vehicle relative to the atmosphere. It is equivalent to the
body axes rotated through the OY axis by an amount equal to the incidence angle.
It is called sometimes stability axes, however some authors differentiate between
wind and stability axes by considering the last having the OX axis coincident with
the projection of the total airspeed V in the plane of symmety. The stability axes
is used in small disturbance analysis. Figure 2.1 shows the relationship between
body and wind axes.

2.1.2 Aircraft Attitude


The orientation of one reference frame to another can be well dened
                                                                           by three
angles: yaw, pitch and roll angles. They are the rotations required, about the axis
z, y and x, respectively, in order for one 'ame to coincide with the other. For the
special case when the datum is aligned with the earth reference 'ame the attitude
angles are denominated Euler Angles. They are particularly important because
altitude sensors measure Euler Angles. The Euler angles are the attitude
                                                                                   pitch
(19), attitude roll (45) and attitude yaw ( ). Aircraft attitude is also represented by
other angles such as the ight path angle y, which is
                                                             equal to the pitch attitude
angle (9= y) when the aircraft axes are in wind axes, and the aerodynamic angles
obtained from the orthogonal components (u, v,w) of the total
                                                              Velocity Vwhich are
given by;

                                                      13
2.1.4 Controls Sign Conventions


The usual inputs to the aircraft stability and control equations are the control
surface angular displacements.     The basic aircraft control surfaces are the
elevator, ailerons and rudder whose deections are measured normal to the hinge
line. The general rule for control deections and response disturbances is:
negative control surface displacement give rise to positive forces and moments.
Otherwise positive moment in pitch is nose up, in roll is clockwise and in
                                                                               yaw is
nose to the right. lt is assumed also that a positive controller displacement causes
a positive response. For the analysis that follows the port aileron
                                                                      displacement is
taken as the reference value for the denition and signal of the control
                                                                           parameters
related to ailerons. In dening le and right it has been assumed the observer
looking from the rear to the front of the aircra.




2.2 Mathematical Models Formulation


When system identication is applied to an aircraft as a
                                                           dynamic system the
equations goveming its motion must be postulated and the measurement of inputs
and outputs must be considered. I particular, when
                                                   dealing with state estimation
and aerodynamic modelling three sets of mathematical models are
                                                                   required; the
Dynamic Model, the Observatior Model and the Aerodynamíc or Estímatíon
Model.


For the Estimation-Before-Modelling technique, as
                                                congured for this research,
the dynamic model together with the observation model are used in the state
estimation step inside an Extended Kalman Filter. The dynamic model is used for
state propagation from the previous instant of time to the
                                                           present instant of time
and while the observation model is used for state
                                                   update with the measurement
infonnation. The estimation model is used in the parameter estimation
                                                                            phase
(linear regression) and is the model that the engineer or mathematician wants to
fit to the aircraft aerodynamic characteristics.

-
    Dynamic models.                           2
Dynamic models or kinematic models are mathematical equations that describe
the dynamic characteristics of the aircra. They are rst order differential
equations in which applied extemal forces and body rotation rates are forcing
functions and which are nomally described by a vector non-linear differential
equation in the following format;


                             X(f) = f[X(f) +B(f)]                        (2-4)




                                         14
where x(t) is the aircraft state vector and B(t) is the vector of forcing functions.

 - Observatíon models.
Observation models are mathematical expressions that relate on-board measured
variables, such a accelerations, body rotation rates, airspeed, incidence and
sideslip angle, etc, to the components of the aircraft state vector. Representing the
measured variables by the vector z(t) and the aircraft states by the vector x(t) the
measurement model of a state estimation process is commonly represented by;


                                 lm = f[x<fº.f1                           (2.5)

Here the aircra state vector may be formed om the augmented state vector
which may contain more elements than the state vector of an aircra.

-
    Aerodynamic models.
Aerodynamic     models    consist    of   mathematical   modelsrepresenting        the
aerodynamic forces and moments acting on the aircraft written as,


                     y(t) = ®0 + ®lx,+...+®x + ®u1+...+®u,                (2.6)

where y(t) represents a state or its derivative or any one
                                                             component of the
aerodynamic force and moment, (9, to ®, are the stability and control
derivatives. The coefcient 90 corresponding to the initial condition,
                                                                      xl to xâ
are aircra states and u, are control variables.

(viii) Propeller Model and Thnst Measurement Teclmiques

The engine thrust appears in the equations of motion as one of the extemal forces.
It is also an element of the obsewation model indispensable for the identication
of aerodynamic models.       In the EKF formulation the engine thrust will be
included as an input.


For a turboprop aircra, as the HP Jetstream-100, the total
                                                             applied thrust is that
resultant om the propeller and the engine exhaust jet.
                                                                 Propeller thrust
measurement is a difcult subject. There are possibilities such as direct
                                                                         force and
moment measurement using load cells and propeller slipstream measurement
using momentum theory, however, they require special instrumentation which for
the College of Aeronautic's Jetstream is not available, and even so thrust is
determined with marginal accuracy. For the Jetstream case, the
                                                                        only option
available is that based on propeller models, that is, models based on blade element
theory and momentum theory relating operating conditions to a predíction of
free-air propeller perfonnance. Propeller models are
                                                          supplied by the propeller
manufacturers in the fonn of propeller charts. They are theoretical, valid for free-
air, however substantiated by wind-tunnel experimental tests with scaled models.

The H Jetstream-100 aircra is powered by two Astazou
                                                                turboprop engines
driving Hamilton Standard propellers type 23 LF-335.            For normal ight
conditions the engine rotation is set at 40300 RPM and the
                                                              propeller is geared to
rotate at 1782 RPM. The engine power
                                                setting is controlled/varied by an
automatic fuel control system that, due to its mode of
                                                        operation is known as Beta
control which has authority over the engine for a specied
                                                             envelop. Changes in




                                        21
power setting are reected as changes in blade angles which in tum cause
changes in propeller rotation. Due to non equilibrium condition between the
engine and the propeller torque the fuel controller changes the fuel ow in order
to restore the equilibriun and keep the rotation constant.


For actual applications the engine power and propeller setting model is not well
dened in the aircra manuals.    There is only a limited amount of available
information which allowed to dene two power/propeller models used in
simulation models of the Jetstream, as presented by Desrochers [22] and
Davidson [23]. One of these models gives propeller thrust as a function of the
fuel ow while the other gives the thnst as a function of the
                                                                 airspeed, however,
valid for a narrow band of airspeed.


The propeller models used in the denition of propeller charts are
                                                                  normally
dened in tenns of,
                         -Propeller advance ratio           J,
                         - Power coefcient ............._

                         - Thrust coefcient ...............CT

Engine exhaust jet thrust is available from engine manufacturer supplied chart.
Actual exhaust jet thrust is small if compared with the
                                                        propeller thnst and its
absence of the total installed thrust will not affect
                                                       significantly the overall
accuracy.

In order to validate the propeller efciency, tests can be carried out
                                                                             using a
technique developed by Bull and Bridges [24].            This technique allows the
determination of the propulsive efficiency, parasitic and induced
                                                                    drag coefcients
using the infonnation contained in the transient response in airspeed of the
aircra following a small change of power in level
                                                     flight, Using this technique at
test points close to the ones used for the estimation
                                                              tests, propeller chart
efficiency values may be validated or its values may be multiplied by a correction
factor calculated from the test results. The corrected values
                                                               may reect an overall
efciency that includes the engine exhaust jet thnst and the iteration between
propeller, nacelle, wing, etc., however they are valid for the test condition only.

2.3 Aerodynamic Model and Estimation Model


The previous sections presented the Dynamic and Observatíon models which are
used to estimate the aircraft states, that is, to solve the state estímatíon
                                                                         problem or
the determination of the time dependent elements of the whole identication
process. The parameter estimatíon phase, i.e., the determination of the time
invariant elements of the identication problem requires the denition of a model
for what is desired to estimate. This model constitutes the Estimation Model and
in the present research the objective is the detemiination of aircraft
                                                                       aerodynamic
derivatives. Aerodynamic derivatives, however, are not a sufcient denition
since they can be presented in different ways. The two most common models for
the denition aerodynamic derivatives are the American and British. In this
research the British approach will be used and follows the RE Technical
                                                                               Report
TR 66200 [27].


Let us consider equations 2.11 and 2.12. They are the
                                                        general equations for the
analysis of motion of a rigid body. They are non-linear and so it is useful to
assume further analysis in terms of small perturbation
                                                       principle and linearisation
in order to obtain a set of equations representative of the
                                                             proposed format for
aerodynamic derivatives representation. In principle the aircra is assumed to be
ying in steady state, rectilinear trimmed with no bank angle, sideslip or yaw and
with steady velocity components Ue, V and
                                                 I/Ve. It is assumed that there is no
sideslip so that V = 0. It is further assumed that the aircra experiences small
disturbances in terns of the linear velocities and angular rates. Let u, v and w be
the velocity perturbations in the x, y and z axes ,
                                                      respectively, and p, q and r the
angular rate peturbations in roll, pitch and yaw, respectively. The total velocity
components of the disturbed motion of the aircra cg are,             `

                               V=v    (Ve=0)

such that terms involving products and squares of these
                                                        parameters are very small

2.4 Alternative Models


Models are not absolute and may be fonnulated in order to identify a
                                                                         particular
characteristic.           A
                       model may have a restrictive range of applicability. For
instance, a model may be applicable only to a very narrow range of incidence
angle, altitude, airspeed, etc. Models may have a theoretical fonnulation. This is
the case of the longitudinal and lateral models presented in section 2.3,
                                                                            above,
where the equations have been derived from the analysis of the
                                                                   equilibrium of
forces and moments acting in the body. However, empirical models can be
fomulated too. One such example is where it is suspected that a given variable is
dependent on another and for example a polynomial is used to represent the
model. This approach is used in the identication of aircra
                                                                     aerodynamic
characteristics and one is of the most common applications in the
                                                                  analysis of non-
linear ight conditions.s_uch as high incidence manoeuvers.


The
    next; paragraphs present some" models used in aircra aerodynamic modelling
based on empirical or semi-empirical model fonnulation
                                                        following the American
notation in incidence and sideslip angles:

- An
     equation model common in the literature, e.g. References [2] and [3], is the
representation of the aerodynamic coefcients as multivariable polynomials in
response and control variables (valid for subsonic conditions). The parameters
are the coefcients of the Taylor Series expansion around the values
corresponding to initial steady state ight. Reference [3], in particular, uses the
following representation:




                                                31
(i)    The longitudinal coeffcients C, , C, and C, represented as functions
       Of a,ß, 7,a,q<1,na,0l/º,ß2,0l' (n = 3,--~3)


(ii)   The lateral coefcients Cy, C, and C represented as functions of
       ß,p,r,§,§§a,aß,pa,ra,.§a,§a,ßa2,pa2,rcf,§a2,§'a26,ß3,...ÜZa2,a2,a3.
       The tems Cx, C, ,CZ , C1, C. and Cm are assumed to be known, i.e.,
       are estimated from the measurements of
                                                 aay,a    thrust, p,q,r( and
       derivatives).


(iii) Reference [2] denes a more general expression mainly directed to the
       application for nonlinear conditions,


              c=
                                                 +2                       (2.56)
                             i          j         i   j

which is analogous to the ones presented under (i) and (ii), above.

-
  Alternatively for the mathematical model represented by equations 2.50 and
2.55 one may simply add tenns in a or any other power as well as terms in
powers of ß to the equations, uniquely to ty to increase the model accuracy (to
improve the t). This means terms that have been dropped because the previously
based assumptions that such tenrs were too small are now
                                                              being included
(reconsidered). The models presented this section are particularly applicable to
partitioning analysis and general non-linear models as well.

Parameter estimation methods are usually applied to small manoeuvres executed
by an aircra about some trim ight condition, assuming that a linear
aerodynamic model is valid. However, even for the cases of small perturbations
about a trim angle of incidence a non-linear aerodynamic model
                                                                    may be required,
as in the case when the trim condition is in or near a region of
                                                                    rapidly changing
aerodynamic characteristics, as near stall. Cases where manoeuvres involve large
Variations in incidence, sideslip, or control position, a non-linear model is almost
always necessary.      The analysis of large manoeuvers shows that a proper
application of estimation techniques can reveal an aerodynamic model that would
otherwise require many small manoeuvre.         This leads to the data partition
procedure, i.e., dividing the manoeuvre that covers a large range of some variable
into several partitions. Each of the partitions spans a smaller
                                                                    range of that
variable and produces a model valid for that variable
                                                         range. Despite its narrow
range of validity such a models may identify non-linearities that would be
imperceptible for the conventional analysis and may be very simple, however,
useful when expanding ight envelop into a unknown
                                                    region.




                                            32
                                   Chapter 3



                            STATE ESTIMATION



3.1 General Concepts

In principle, parameter identication may be perfomed without
                                                                       prior state
estimation other than to transforrn measured parameters to desired
                                                                   dependent and
independent variables to be used in the linear regression process. However,
before modelling aircra aerodynamics it is extremely important to
                                                                      identify the
aircraft states accurately. Signicant errors may affect the measurements and
must be identified before the data is used in any calculation. Otherwise, direct
measurements of certain important dynamic variables may be
                                                                     inpractical or
unreliable to be perfonned.      States like the ow measured ones (airspeed,
incidence and sideslip angles), are highly susceptible to measurement errors due
to the nature of their sensors and are of particular importance for the
                                                                        analysis of
quasi-steady-state and dynamic ight conditions.

The application of state estimation methodology to aircra is
                                                               easily accomplished
because the forces and resulting motion of an aircra are related
                                                                    by well-known
equations of motion based mainly on inertial and gravitational models. What is
actually done is to calculate all states based on a set of equations that represent
aircraft dynamics and a set of measurements whose errors are assumed to be
known. This approach is called also 'path reconstruction' or
                                                              'compat`b`l`ty check'
and may be perfonned both in a detenninistic or a probabilistic sense.


There are several different techniques for state estimation.
                                                               However, due to the
characteristics of the models and equations involved, the
                                                           availability of redundant
measurements and the possibility to include process and measurement noise to
the analysis, the Extended Kalman Filter is
                                              particularly attractive and has been
extensively used in two step estimation process, e. g. References [8],[l7],[l 8].

The Extended Kalman Filter (EKF) is an approximate solution to the non-linear
estimation/ltering problem. I the EKF the linear Kalman lter equations are
applied to the non-linear problem through a set of linear perturbation equations
that describe the deviation of the state 'om a reference
                                                              state, which may be
dened a priori or simply based on the estimated state itself.

3.3 Extended Kalman Filter


The optimal state estimates of the augmented state vector
                                                        represented by equation
3.12 and modeled by the dynamic and measurement models as
                                                             presented above in
sections 2.2.1 and 2.2.2 are obtained by an application of an Extended Kalman
Filter (for continuous dynamics and discrete
                                                  measurements).     The lter
implementation has been performed by following the scheme described by Gelb
[29]. The principal computations comprising the EKF algorithn are outlined as
follows;

- Initial
         Cora''tíons;
To stat the computations of the estimates the initial values of the state
                                                                      variables
are required. For the measured states they are obtained from recorded trim data
immediately prior to the commencement of the dynamic manoeuvre, and for
unmeasured states initial values have to be attributed based on
                                                                 expected values
for these states. When the ight condition
                                          immediately prior to the manoeuvre of
interest is an out of trim dynamic condition, better
                                                        starting values may be
obtained through the use of a fixed point
                                           smoothing lter in order to improve
initial estimates.




                                        37
Initial state covariance matrix P(0) may be assumed equal to the measurement
noise for the measured parameters.    For umneasured parameters a value of 0
equal to one-fourth of the estimated initial value of the state may be assumed,
thus PU
       =(:1-?,..)2. In the absence of any a priori knowledge, it is common to
assume a fairly high value for PL, (0).


- Process and Measurement Noise;
Measurement noise is obtained from the analysis of the time histories of the
measured trim data immediately prior to the commencement of the dynamic
manoeuvre following a procedure described by Bauer and Andrisani [30]. Over
the time histories of each measured variable, bounds are drawn
                                                               dening a noise
envelope representing +/ -la (+/- one standard deviation) about the local mean.
A tenn R of the noise covariance matrix R (assumed
                                                          diagonal) is given by
Rigi'

=Theprocess noise matrix Q is assumed to be diagonal and it is
                                                               very difficult to
dene its elements. For measured parameters the initial values of
                                                                 process noise
may be assumed to be of the order of 10 to 1000 times their respective
measurements noise. For an unmeasured parameter a guess has to be made. Final
values are obtained iteratively by lter 'tuning' which is carried out
                                                               by varying the
values of the process noise and observing the residual sequences of the lter
operation, the convergence of the lter gain and covariance and the characteristic
of the estimates.


Tuning is also carried out with the initial covariances P(0), associated with the
initial value of the states, in order to assure that the lter final estimates are not
being affected by them.

- State and Covariance

integration algorithm, thus,

Filter Convergence Ird'cat`ons: to ensure that the nal estimates are as valid as
the data allows, several lter convergence indications must be
                                                                  checked;

(i) Inspection of the time histories of the parameter estimates and of the
    covariance matrix evolution, with the desired result being a visible
    asyrptotic convergence prior to the end of the data record.




                                       42
(ii) Inspection of the cross-correlation terms of the nal covariance matrix after
     matrix nonnalisation by variances (diagonal terms).      Off-diagonal terms
    approaching unity indicate uniqueness and/or diffculties in the identiability
    of the respective parameter. It is expected nonnalised off-diagonal tenns
    approaching zero.


(iii) Inspection of lter residual sequences. They must appear to be white and
      consistent with their calculated statistics. Dispersion serves as a guide in
    verifying that particular tenns of matrices Q and R were selected
    correctly. Residual drift from zero may indicate modelling errors. A test
    for whíteness can be based on the analysis of the autocorrelation
    function of the measurement residual. Assuming that the residual
    (innovation) is a random process stationary and ergodic an estimation
    of the autocorrelation function ça can be calculated by;

    that theoretically range between plus one and minus one.    If the cross-

    diagonal elements;

                                    p"") = ml)

triangular and D diagonal is more important to identify near singularity (nearly
observable states) than the analysis of the covariance matrix P. While small
variances are sufficient to cause near singularity they are not a necessary
condition, however, a small element in D is a clear indication of near singularity.




3.4 State Smoothing



3.4.1 Modified Bryson-Frazier Smoother.


In order to reduce the bias that is inherent with the Extended Kalman Filter,
because it is only a approximate linear lter (Klein [19]), it is usual to
incorporate a linear data smoothing lter in series with the EKF. The smoother
that is most 'equently identied in the literature is the Modied Bryson-Frazier
Smoother (MBF) which uses adjoint variables generated from a reverse Kalman-
like lter.   This smoother is largely used due to its effectiveness. Its peculiar
format and commonality with the Kalman Filter allows to store the Kalman
                                                                            gain,
Covariance Matrix and other tenns generated by the EKF to be used
                                                                    subsequently
by the backward smoother. The adjoint variables generated 'om the reverse time
lter are recursive and are used to produce the smoothed states and covariances.


The computational steps involved in its execution are presented below and
follows Biennan [33],

3.4.2 Fixed lag Differentiator-smoother


I aircraft parameter identication procedures like the E-B-M method it is
necessary at some stage to differentiate the motion variables recorded in ight or
data estimated by a lter. The variable data inevitably includes various levels of
noise even in the case of data estimated by a lter. The differentiation of
                                                                                  noisy
data is always a difcult task even when the data is characterized
                                                                         by high signal-
to-noise ratio.                               u
There are many existing techniques for numerical differentiation.           One of the
most accurate     and   more   commonly used      methods     requires    a two   stage
computation process comprising;


(i)    data ltering to improve infonnation quality of the tne signal,
(ii)   derivative computation by numerical differentiation.

A new approach to the problem, recently published
                                                  by Fioretti and Jetto [34], is
based on state space fonnulation in which smoothed estimate of the data and its
derivatives are computed simultaneously by means of a Fixed
                                                                 Lag smoothing
algorithm. Important characteristics of the new technique are that it is generally
applicable to any signal data and that the noise characteristics of the data are
determined by the technique prior to the application of the
                                                                       smoothing-
differentiation procedure.



3.4.2.1 Data Model


Before an attempt can be made to differentiate sampled data, it is
                                                                      required to
dene     a model    representing the data characteristics.     To have general
applicability it is required that assumptions regarding model characteristics
should be valid for a large class of signals. For the model fonnulated
                                                                       by Fioretti
and Jetto [34] only two assumptions are made;


(i)    The sampled data is assumed to be a stochastic process, continuously
       differentiable up to a suitable order n.


(ii)   The derivative of order n+l is assumed to be a zero-mean white process
       ~N
           (0, 02), i.e., Gaussian and regular enough to be differentiable up to
       the rth derivative.




                                         46
These assumptions lead to the following state equation to represent the data
model,


                       x((k+1)A) = A,x(/(Af) + w((k+1)A)                      (3.41)

where
                                             2                    n

3.4.2.3 Fixed Lag Smoother


Numerical experiments carried out with the model described in section 3.3.2.1
and optimal noise characteristics as determined in section 3.3.2.2 show that a
Kalman lter nomally does not estimates state derivatives with the desired
accuracy. In general the derivatives are still noisy. The natural solution to this
difculty is the use of a smoother and in a rst instance a xed-interval smoother
is sought.      Moreover, the xed-interval smoother is computationally
disadvantageous because the best algorithms involve backward calculation
requiring a lot of computer storage and time. The xed-lag is a satisfactory
altemative and in practice provides estimates with the same
                                                             degree of accuracy
[35].

An easy way to implement a xed-lag smoother is
                                                       augmenting the state vector
with additional states representing the values of the states in the previous instant
of time. The additional states may be represented
                                                   by,

                                  º=i<iº=º«<f-1)
                                  x2(`) = x1(' - 1)
                                         ºâ                              (3 54)

                                  X1: X1-(11)

Thus the additional vectors contains the l previous values of the state vector
                                                                                  x(')
where l is the time lag in sampling intervals,
                                                       resulting in the following
augmented state space system:

            º.            ..         ..       º.       ..        .    ..        ..

3.5 Reconstructibility Analysis


The reconstructibility of a linear state estimation
                                                problem may be evaluated by an
analytical process detennining in this way what can effectively be calculated for
the system dynamic model and the assumed obsevation model. This
                                                                         analysis




                                          52
allows to identify which observation model would produce the best estimates for
those cases where available measurements would pennit to formulate several
different sets of observation models. I a state estimation problem in general not
all components of the state vector will be reconstructible and
                                                               many will be
reconstructed with errors whose magnitude may be identified by the
reconstructibility analysis.
Consider the linear stochastic system;


                             i = Fx(t) + Bí(t) + Gwx
                             i= Hx(t)+Jü(t)+wz                          (364)

Where x is the n x 1 state vector, í is the m x 1 input vector, z is the r x 1
observation vector, i.e., the observation model. The vectors wx and
                                                                       wz denotes,
respectively, the process and the measurement noise of the represented system It
is assumed that the matrices F,B,H and J are perfectly known.
I order to identify the reconstructible subspace of x it is
                                                            required to write the
reconstructibility matrix QR for the system represented by equation 3.64. The
matrix QR is given by;
                                       H
                                         H.F

                               QR =      11.112                         (3.65)


                                      H.F""


The   system   is   completely reconstructible if the row vectors of the
reconstructibility matrix QR span the n-dimensional space. The unreconstructible
subspace of the system is the null space of the reconstructibility matrix QR and is
invariant with F, Kwakemaak and Sivan [45].
If nl is the maximum number of independent rows in
                                                      QR, thus the reconstructible
subspace is of dimension nl.       A matrix U of rank n, partitioned, can be
constmcted from QR,


                                       U1

                                           U2

                                                  QR and U2 is the basis for the
unreconstructible subspace of dimension n-nl. From the matrix U it is
                                                                         possible
to determine a vector x', partitioned in two vectors, the nal reconstructible
                                                                              (x)
and the unreconstructible (xf,) vectors;




                                           53
                                     X:    U,
                                    --- = --- .x

                                      x;          U2


Observe that QR is analogous to the observability matrix of a system. More
precisely it is the transpose of the observation matrix.

Instead of determining a unique matrix Q R for the system, several matrices Q
                                                                             R,
are determined, each one associated to one row H, of the observation matrix H.
From each matrix
                 QR, it is possible to derive the reconstructible subspace of the
state vector x associated with the measurement         z,.. This is obtained by
multiplying QRí by x.
