                                          60
The reconstructible subspace for multiple observations can be obtained by
combíning the subspaces due to individual observations. For instance, combíning
u and q observations the reconstructed subspace will contain now u,q, M,1\/4,1%
while 6 is reconstructed with a error that is a function of bx.  The nal
unreconstructible subspace contains w,b,b,. Combining the observations u,a
and 6 allows to reconstruct all the states of the identication problem.   I this
case the addition of q to the observation model does not add any new information
to the state estimation problem.


The extension of the analysis to a linear lateral model is straightforward,
however, it is much more laborious. Note that in equations 3.69 to 3.74 the
accelerations ax and az were used as inputs replacing the representations ofX and
Z as second order Gauss-Markov models in order to reduce the number of states
(by six) allowing to demonstrate the procedure.      For the lateral case, however,
only Y can be replaced by the measurement ay and L and N must be modelled by
second order Gauss-Markov models as M was in the longitudinal case. Under
these circumstances the lateral model will have eighteen states
                                                                  making almost
impossible to demonstrate      by hand    calculation the entire matrix algebra
manipulation. Anyway, no reconstructibility problems are envisaged. Mulder
[43] shows that for a linear lateral model with state equations in /3,45, ,y ie.,
sideslip, attitude roll, attitude yaw and geographical position coordinate, with
input tenns in lateral acceleration, rate of roll and yaw, for an observation model
with sideslip and roll angle, the unreconstructible subspace contains
                                                                       only the bias
of the lateral accelerometer plus the calibration constants of the
                                                                     sideslip angle.
For accelerometer with small bias the reconstructibility is almost
                                                                   complete.

The extension of the reconstnctibility analysis to the complete state estimation
problem proposed in this research is impossible because the estimation model is
non-linear and the analysis is applicable to linear system only. However, based
on the results of the above presented analysis a parallel
                                                            analysis may be made.
The non-linear model contains the same unknowns as the
                                                             longitudinal and lateral
models together. This insures that at least the states of the non-linear model will
be reconstructed as those of the linear one. The non-linear
                                                              model, introduces,
however, additional relations between the states in the dynamic model as well as
in the measurement model. This increases the
                                                      redundancy of information
increasing the condcnce in some results. For example, introducing the altitude
as an additional state as well as a measurement, improves the
                                                              reconstructibility of
w and at the same time reduces the dependence on incidence which is difcult to
measure. Therefore, accurate altitude measurements,
                                                        mainly altitude rates, are
more easily obtained. Using calibrated altitude, incidence and
                                                               sideslip angles but
keeping only bias parameters to these parameters reduce the reconstructibility
problems to the denition of the bias of the lateral accelerometer. However, no



                                         61
analysis can be carried out to deñne the reconstructibility of the lateral
accelerometer bias. In this case the only envisaged solution is to use an accurate
accelerometer or include to the problem a model of the accelerometer bias which
increases problem complexity. Anyway several works in this area, such as those
of Stengel [8] and Stalford [l8], used similar models and no
                                                                 identiability
problems were reported.




                                       62
                                         Chapter 4



  LINEAR REGRESSION I AIRCRAFT PARAMETER ESTIMATION



4.1 Least Square Estimates


Linear regression is used to dene a functional relationship between a dependent
variable and a set of independent variables. It is assumed that the dependent
variable can be approximated by a linear combination of the independent
variables. For the identication of aircraft state and control parameters the
mathematical model of aerodynamic forces and moments may be represented by,


                            y(t) :(90 +®x(t)+"°+®-xn-(f)                         (4-1)

Here the independent variable y(t)         may represent any one of the three
aerodynamic forces X Y and Z or any one of the three moments L, M or N. The
elements x1(), x2(t), ..., x_1(t) are the aircraft states and control variables, their
derivatives or any combination of such a variables, linear or non-linear, at a
                                                                           given
instant in time. The dependent variable y(t) may also be a state derivative as in
the case of modelling a short period mode approximation or a
                                                                   phugoid mode
approximation. The coefcients ®1,...,®_, are the stability and control
derivatives and ®0 corresponds to the initial steady-ight conditions.


For a sequence of observations (m measurements) of y and
                                                               x,. (í=1,2,..n-1) the
resultant set of equations may be expressed in matrix fonn and since
                                                                       equation 4.1
is only an approximation of the actual aerodynamic
                                                        relationship a tenn e is
included to the equation right hand side in order to reect the error
                                                                          (equation
error).


                    y( 1)     1   x1()   x2()     '''   xn-()     G0        er
                    y(2)      1   x(2)   x2(2)    " '   xn-(2)    91        e
                    y(3)    = 1   xçs)   x2(3)    '''   xn-(3)    62      + Q    (4-2)

measurements by a least-square technique in which the square of the
                                                                     equation
error is minimized. For the system identication of aircraft one can determine




                                            63
the stability and control derivatives by a simple regression process by modelling
conveniently the aircra aerodynamics.

The properties of the least square estimates depend upon postulated assumptions
conceming the equations error characteristics.           Let A be the matrix of the
measured independent variables and e the                 error   vector.   The    following
assumptions are made,


(i)     e is uncorrelated with the A.
(ii)    e has zero mean value and variance og.
(iii)   e is a stationary vector and identically distributed.
(iv)    A is measured without error.

4.2 Analysis of Residuals


To improve the ft of a model implies trying to reduce the sum of the
                                                                       squares of
the residuals. This is nonnally accomplished by trying to add terms to the model.
When a variable is added to a model, it may be decomposed into two
                                                                       orthogonal
components a follows;


                                      xz = cxl + 5,1                            (4.12)

It is known that only
                       5,2 , the orthogonal component of xz, may contribute to the
reduction of the residuals. This means that when a variable
                                                              xl is being added to
the model it would be replaced by its orthogonal component and the result in the
regression would be the same.
The term c in equation 4.12 can be calculated om
                                                       xl and xl. To do this x, is
assumed as a vector of dependent variable while xl may be considered as the
matrix of the independent variables of a regression
                                                    allowing to write,


                                 c=[xlTxl]"xlTx2                '               (4.13)

From this, it follows that the orthogonal component
                                                           may be written as,


                              5,2 = x, - xl[xlTxl]"xlTx2                        (4.14)

The new matrix of independent variables may now be
                                                                expressed in partitioned
form as,


                                 x=[xl           5,2]                           (4.15)

The least square estimates of the now modied model
                                                             may be calculated by;

4.3 Stepwise Regression


The Stepwise Regression is a procedure that inserts
                                                      independent variables into a
regression model. Each time a new variable is added to the model, a regression
is perfomed and the t is exanined in order to
                                                 identify whether, or not, the new
variable improves the ñt, when compared with the model of the
                                                                          previous
iteration. The set of variables which produce the best t will dene the model
                                                                                (or
best model) and the resulting coeicients will be the nal coefñcients of the
regression.




                                         67
    The insertion order of the independent variables is detennined
                                                                        by the use of the
    partial correlation coeicient, as a measure of the importance of the variables in
    the overall regression. The partial correlation coefcient is
                                                                 given by,


                                                                              (4.22)
                                           (ssyy )
    where
l

                                  _s. = Z[x,(f)-5.12                          (4.23)

l                                 s = Z[y(f)-712


    and i. is the average value of the independent variable
                                                            j.

    At every regression step the variables incorporated into the model in the
                                                                               previous
    stages as well as the variable entering in the present stage are examined using the
    F- statistic test. A variable may be retained or
                                                     rejected om the model depending
    on the value of the partial F, as calculated by
                                                        equation 4.10. By this means a
    variable may be included or rejected and even a variable entered in the model in a
    previous stage may be rejected if it results in a poor t. The process of selecting
    and checking continues until no more variables can be introduced and no more
    can be rejected. However, there are some additional criteria that
                                                                         may be used in
    the detemination of the best model ft as explained in the
                                                                 following sections.



    4.4 Modified Stepwise Regression


    The mathematical representation of aircra forces and moments is
                                                                              normally
    presented as a set of linearised equations whose coefcients are the aerodynamic
    derivatives. This representation is good for some
                                                          applications. However, the
    interest in post stall and spin ights has created the need to extend
                                                                            parameter
    estimation into ight areas where non-linear
                                                    aerodynamic eifects become more
    pronounced. The stepwise regression method seems to be a ideal tool for
    analysing data of ights in non-linear aerodynamic conditions because it is easy
    to implement and can model terms that would be non-linear in the
                                                                         equations of
    motion but that are still linear for the
                                                regression process.   The concept of
    Modified Stepwise Regression has been introduced by Klein et al [5] meaning
    that the linear terms are introduced rst to the model and
                                                                 may not be removed
    from the aircra/regression model. I other words the modication
                                                                         introduces a
    constraint preventing the removal of linear terms
                                                           regardless of their partial
    correlation coeicients F. All the linear terms are entered and examined rst in



                                            68


    l
    =Ä±
an order according to their partial correlation coefñcients. After all linear terms
are included then non-linear terms are analysed,
                                                      being included or rejected
according to their signicance and the signicance of all the tenns already
included in the model.

4.6 Choice of the Partial 'F Values


The tabulated values of F(1 ,m-n, a
                                  P) are dependent on the number of samples, the
number of parameters in the model and the chosen risk level
                                                                 ap. For m > 100,
the effect of n on F is small ; therefore, F(1,m-n,
                                                     0.01) is taken as 7, regardless
of m and n. The tabulated values of
                                      F(n-1,m-n,ap) for m > 100 and a= 0.01
vary approximately from 3.0 to 2.3. It is indicated by Klein [3] that the observed
F-values should not only exceed the percentage point of the F-distribution but
should be about four times the selected percentage point. Klein
                                                                    [3] also denes
12 as an acceptable value for F in aircra parameter estimation while Hinds
                                                                                 [9]
denes 4 and Hess & Ly [7] denes 5.


Experience with test computation showed that a model based only on the
statistical signicance of individual parameters in the
                                                        regression equation can
still have too many parameters, Mulder et al [4]. Then, more
                                                             quantities and their
values should be analysed a possible criteria for the selection of an
                                                                        adequate
model. Among the quantities that would be examined the
                                                              following may be
considered, Klein [3];


(i) The computed value of F for each parameter considered in the model.
    Since F is the inverse of parameter variance, it should have the
    maximum value for a adequate model.


(ii) The computed value of F. The model that has the maximum F-value may be
     the best because F is given as the ratio of regression mean
                                                                 square to the
     residual mean square.




                                        71
(iii)R2 , the correlation coefñcient, is no longer the most commonly used
    parameter as the measure of the ñt con'ectness (1 = perfect t).


(iv) The value of the residual sum of squares (RSS) dened by equation 4.25.


(v) The residual variance s2(e) = RSS /(m-n) which should be compared
    with an unbiased estimate of the variance o1(e), if available.


(vi) In a adequate model, the time history of the residuals should be close to a
     random sequence, uncorrelated and Gaussian.


(vii) The predicted sum of squares is also a good criterion to use,


                             PRESS                                      (4.31)

4.7 Identiability of Aerodynamic Models


Aerodynamic    models    represent the  aerodynamic forces and moments as
polynomials whose independent variables are the states of the aircraft, their
combinations or their derivatives. For a small perturbation 'om a nominal
                                                                           ight
condition order one polynomials are used while for
                                                      large amplitude high order
terms or non-linear models are considered.         The identication of these
polynomials in this research is carried out by linear regression. As for the state
estimation step where the problem of
                                        reconstructibility has been formulated on
section 3.4, here the question is whether or not all the tenns of the
                                                                       polynomials
can be identied by the linear regression
                                          using the reconstructed states.

Representing the polynomials by,


                                     y=    AC-)+e                      (4.32)

may be further written that;


                                 E{e(i)} = 0

                                                                   identiability
problem.

In general, identiability can be dened as whether or not and to what extent
numerical values for the model parameters can be extracted for the available
ight data. In practice the model complexity is limited by the identiability.

4.9 Collinearity


If the linear dependence reduces the identiability of the estimation
                                                                    process the
problem of collinearity, or near linear dependence among variables, causes
inaccuracies of the estimates.   Collinearity can inate the variances of the
regression coefñcients, magnify the eects of errors in the regression variables
and produce unstable numerical results.


For    the   analysis
                   of collinearity, rst assume that the regression data
(measurements) are centered and scaled to unit length as dened by Draper and
Smith [36]. I this case the matrix [ATA] is the n x n matrix of correlations. If

AfAK = 0,1' ± k, where j,k are columns of A, then the regressors are orthogonal.
Otherwise, the vectors A A 2,...,A are called linearly dependent if there is a set
of scalars a not all zero such that;

                                        I
                                        Z,.A,. = o                          (4.43)
                                        j=Ä±

I actual application the relation above, equation 4.43, is
                                                            only approximate, that
is, it is a small value, however not zero, which indicates near linear dependency
in the matrix A. So, collinearity exists and [ATA] is ill-conditioned which can
cause computational problems and reduction in the
                                                       accuracy of the estimates.

Collinearity may be caused by three different sources;


(i)    insuñicient excitation of at least one of the variables of the regressor.
(ii)   data constraints caused by a inherent characteristics of the system, eg,
       simultaneous deection of various control surfaces due to the action of a
      stability augmentation system.
(iii) inadequacy of the regression model. For example, model overparametrized.



                                            75
Despite eventual computational problems, collinearity causes large absolute
values for the parameter estimates and large values for the
                                                              corresponding
covariances. It may be detected by the following procedures;


(i)    analysis of the correlation matrix [A'A].
(ii)   by singular value decomposition and eigensystem analysis.
(iii) variance decomposition.

A high correlation coefñcient between two
                                               regressors is a good indication that
collinearity exists, low correlation, however, does not indicate the absence of the
problem. The correlation analysis is not sufficient to ensure the non existence of
collinearity and so additional analysis is required.

The regression is said to be collinear if there exists a non zero vector v such that
Av z 9, where . is a constant vector. To measure how far the matrix A is 'om
exact collinearity it is required to nd a matrix a such that A +a is
                                                                      exactly
collinear. The spectral norm of a can be used for this
                                                       purpose,


                                   Ha" =
                                           r"nHz:xl|av||                      (4.44)


Therefore, ||a|| is an absolute distance measure that does not take into account the
size of A. However, this problem can be solved
                                                      by measuring the distance om
A to the exact collinearity relative to the size of A. This leads to the
                                                                         measure,


                                           Nl
                                           "A"                                (4.45)

Small values of the ratio (4.45) indicates that the columns of A are collinear. The
inverse of equation 4.45 is comnonly known a a condition number or condition
index of A,

                                    c=
                                                                              (4.46)


Large value of K indicates that A is collinear and the matrix is said to be ill-
conditioned.


The condition number of A is also related to the
                                                           singular values (SV) of A and
hence to the eigenvalues of ATA because the
                                                concepts of SVD and eigenvalues
eigenvectors are closely related and for eigenvalues and singular values expressed
as Ä and c it is possible to show (see Golub and Van Loan

There are many possibilities to treat the problem of
                                                         collinearity. I principle
collinearity is not a statistic problem but instead a data problem. Among the
possibilities the simplest is to redene the regression model while the most
traumatic would be to redene the test by
                                              improving the input/manoeuvre and
repeating the test. The best approach to avoid collinearity is probably to plan
carefully the design of the aircraft manoeuvre and to plan the model to be used in
the regression. Chapter 5 presents some
                                         concepts of identiability associated with
input design which can be extended to collinearity. Mathematically it is possible
to use a altemative estimation
                                    technique such a principal component
regression, mixed estimation and ridge estimation which are altemative and
biased forms of the ordinary least square procedure.
                                                       Ridge regression is the
subject of Appendix D.

4.10 New Approach to the Least Square Linear
                                                             Regression

When estimating parameters using the equation error method one
                                                                     way to access
the accuracy of the estimates is through the
                                                  analysis of the variance matrix
generated by the least square procedure or by repeating the experiment and
observing the scatter of the results. It is common to observe that despite the
apparent high accuracy of the estimates demonstrated by the covariance matrix
the dispersion of the results is contradictorily
                                                 high. It is well known that this
controversy is caused by the incapacity of the least square method to take into
account errors in the independent variables. In
                                                 principle the method asstunes that
all errors are conned to the observation vector of the
                                                          dependent variable. This
assumption, however, is unrealistic because due to different error sources it may
be impossible to know exactly the independent variables.
                                                         Trying to overcome this
drawback a new fornulation to the least square method was introduced in 1980
by Golub and Van Loan [47] called Total Least Square.

Total Least Square


The least square (LS) estimation problem may be formulated as
                                                     follows;
Given an m x n data matrix A with mzn and a m vector
                                                     y, nd ( as the
solution to


                                      mm       ||e||
                                                                                (455)
                                      s.t.    AG) = y + e

The implicit assumption here is that errors
                                                    only occurs in the vector y and that the
matrix A is perfectly known. Thus the LS
                                         problem corresponds to disturbing the
observation y by a minimum amount e so that y+ e can
                                                     by predicted by A.

One way to take into account the errors in A is to introduce
                                                             perturbations also in
A and consider the following modied
                                      problem;

                               mim           A,e"
                               s.t.                                             (4.56)
                                        (A+A)®=y+e

where    denotes the Euclídean nonn or F robenius matrix nonn.
If a minimizing pair (Ã,ê) is found for
                                             equation 4.56 then any Ö satisfying
(A + A)G) = y+ ê is said to solve the Total Least Square (TLS) problem.

                                  INPUT ANALYSIS



5.1 General Concepts


The determination of aircra aerodynamic and control parameters 'om
                                                                           ight test
data involves three interrelated problems;

 - aircraft
          instrumentation,
 - denition of a calculation or extraction
                                             procedure for the determination of the
   aerodynamic derivatives,
 - denition of
               adequate inputs to control surfaces, i.e., adequate manoeuvres.

For the present research the instrmnentation has been assumed to be
                                                                          xed, i.e.,
the one installed and available in the Craneld
                                                  University College of Aeronautics
Jetstream aircra.      The method to be used for aerodynamic derivatives
calculation is the equation error in the form of a two
                                                       step approach or Estimation-
Before-Modelling technique. It is required, however, the specication of the
input(s) format to be used in the aircraft excitation suitable for aerodynamic
model structure determination for the research case
                                                      study.

From the rst attempts to apply parameter estimation
                                                  techniques to ight test data
analysis users identied that to have good estimates it was important to plan
carefully for the manoeuvres, choosing appropriate control inputs and exciting
specic aircra modes. Many different control inputs have been tested and it was
recognized that input fonnat should inuence not only the accuracy but also the
identiability of the estimates.   Good inputs could solve or reduce parameter
identiability or collinearity problems and improve condence of estimates of
stability and control derivatives obtained 'om ight test data.

The selection of inputs (control surface deections as a function of
                                                                      time) to excite
a specic mode requires several considerations;


(i) Instrumentation Iimitation.
The instruments dynamic range and their
                                              signal-to-noise ratio characteristics
impose constraints on the type and magnitude of the ight test manoeuvres. The
manoeuvre must be inside the dynamic range of the instrtunentation and must be
well identied despite the signal-to-noise ratio characteristics of the
                                                                       signals. The
signal-to-noise ratio is a primary factor for the requirement of large amplitude




                                        82
manoeuvre. For the noise independent of the manoeuvre size,
                                                                   large manoeuvre
results in large signal-to-noise ratio and better estimates. Maine and Iliff
                                                                                [40]
conclude that signals should have a signal-to-noise ratio of at least 10 to
                                                                            produce
good estimates. Thus signal-to-noise ratio may impose a lower bound to
manoeuvre amplitude.                                                             .


(ii) Model assumption.
The input must be coherent with the assumed aircra
                                                   aerodynanic model. For a
linearised, decoupled model the selected input     should be such that the
assumptions of linearity and decoupling are not violated. Therefore, even using a
complex six-degree-of-freedom non-linear model the simultaneous estimation of
all parameters of the model from one single manoeuvre
                                                        may be not feasible due to
non uniqueness and identiñability problems. It is
                                                      important to remember that
despite the formulation of a complex non-linear model, the mathematical solution
almost always assumes local linearisation, as in the case of a Extended Kalman
Filter.


(iii) Parameter Sensítiviy.
For obtaining good estimates 'om ight test data it is
                                                              important that the
measured response of the aircra should be sensitive to the
                                                              parameters that are
being identied. It is a mathematical problem of observability and identiability,
which is not easy to formulate and solve if the formulated model is non-linear.


(iv) Pilot Acceptabílity
The input should be a simple a possible and should be
                                                               capable of being
implemented by the pilot. The aircraft response should not endanger the pilot and
the aircraft safety.


(v) Input Power
The input power should satisfy both the Model
                                              Assumption and the Parameter
Sensitivity. Large amplitude input is in general not applicable to linear model
while low amplitude input may not excite the mode to be
                                                          identied, mainly when
a non-linear characteristic is being
                                     investigated. Otherwise it is very difcult to
design a adequate large amplitude manoeuvre. It is important to remember that
power input demand may satisfy the limits of the aircra actuators and that the
aircraft must be operated inside its ight
                                             envelope which is normally restricted
during ight test campaigrs. Parameter estimation tests are in general associated
with aircra ight envelop expansion.


In general the power of a input
                                   signal should be well distributed over a wide
range of frequencies. I Figure 5.1, 'om reference [44], the power spectral
densities (PSD) of three inputs are presented. For the
                                                       multiple input case like a



                                       83
'32l 1' the PSD has a relatívely wide band whereas the doublet excites a
                                                               particular
band at a higher equency and the step input contains
                                                     energy only at low
'equencies.


              _                                                          INPUT SIGNALS:



                  \`                                                     ---oouster
                    \                                                                     -

                                    Figure 5.1 PSD of three different inputs.



(iv) Identability
The tmknown parameters of a system are identiable if it is
                                                               possible to identify
their values based on the measurements of the
                                                        system input and output
parameters. It is common to talk about system identiability, however, the
denition depends inherently on the parametrization of the
                                                                system. It is more
reasonable to say that parameters of a system are identiable than to
                                                                       say that the
system is identiable because system can be made identiable by specifying
known values for enough of its parameters.
                                             Anyway it is possible to demonstrate
system with parameters that are unidentiable, e.g., systems with no
                                                                           outputs.
Fortunately this is not the case for aircra nor of the
                                                       equations used to represent
aircraft motion.


Manoeuvre design and identiability are related
                                                                   subjects and the denition of
one cannot be made without
                                             considering the other. Manoeuvres can be designed
with good identiability by
                            following some general guidelines, as the followings
(plus the ones presented above);

 -
     Independent inputs.
Inputs must be independent for every control surface for which it is intended to
estirnate derivatives. When the motion of one control surface is
                                                                       connected




                                                                84
(proportional) to the motion of a other surface then it will be impossible to
distinguish the effects of each individual sturface. This is typically the case of
aileron and rudder interconnection where due to the proportional movement only
a equivalent combined derivative can be determined, ignoring how much of the
effect comes 'om each individual surface. Therefore, if one rudder
                                                                          pulse is
added to the manoeuvre after the lateral stick pulse then both derivatives can be
evaluated with no degradation despite the interconnection between aileron and
rudder.

  - Modal excitation.    The input must excite the mode(s) under analysis and
represented by the model and must not excite, or if so, produce minimum
excitation to unmodelled modes. If the model chosen to get the
                                                               input-output data
is inadequate the parameters of the model may be forced to account for some
unmodelled effects resulting in estimated parameter values quite different from
what aerodynamic theory and previous results may indicate. Thus the
                                                                        design of
a appropriate input starts by selecting the control surface, which has almost no
options in the case of aircraft, and nish by dening the shape of the input signal.

  - Excitation
                Frequency Content.      The system modes must be excited by
frequencies close to their natural 'equencies. A ideal input must have a large
spectrum. It must cover the range of natural frequencies of the system. Maine
and Iliff [40] denes a 'equency sweep a a obvious
                                                       signal to meet equency
requirements dening also the '32l 1' as a good alternative, easy to execute and
which produces good results.

  - Feedback.
                Identiability problems occur in aircraft equipped with a
automatic feedback system for two different reasons;


    1- difculty to excite the airc-raft due to the action of the
                                                              system. The aircraft
motion decays without oscillation when the input ceases - it is the reason for the
feedback system.    A solution is to tum off the system or reduce its gain or,
altematively the input should persist for almost the entire duration of the
manoeuvre, e. g., a series of '32l l' works better than one input alone [40].

   2- the feedback system introduces linear
                                             dependence between control surface
position and states. I this case independent inputs must be input to each control
surface that moves during the manoeuvre.


When control surface positions are inputs to the model then the calculated
derivatives are open-loop derivatives independent of the action of the feedback
system whose actual effect is to change the input characteristics, not to change
model's inner structure [40].




                                         85
It is difficult to define or design an input.   Maine and Iliff [40] states that input
design is the most difcult task of aircra parameter estimation. The inputs most
commonly      encountered in the literature are the step, doublet, '32l 1' and
'equency sweep. They are generally used to identify aircra parameters in both
linear and non-linear ight regimes. However, the estimates of certain
                                                                      parameters
may be quite poor and, in some cases, a set of parameters may not be identiable
at all requiring additional ights with modied inputs in order to better
                                                                         identify
the desirable set of parameters.


The search for estimates with improved accuracy led
                                                        many researchers to the
development of optimal input determination techniques where input signals were
optimized with respect to many different criteria, in the time domain or in the
frequency domain. From the early l970's to middle eighties the search for
optimization of control inputs became a particular case study of parameter
estimation.   The use of analytical teclmiques in input design was
                                                                         probably
initiated by Fisher [41] who identied the properties of the infonnation
                                                                          matrix,
M. Later Cramer and Rao [42] showed that the
                                                dispersion matrix, the inverse of
the information matrix, is a bound on parameter error
                                                      covariance, dening in this
way the Cramer-Rao lower bound.        Most of the analytical methods in input
design use a function of the dispersion matrix or of the infonnation matrix in the
denition of the optimization criteria. Basing the optimization of
                                                                  input signals on
a norm of the information matrix results in
                                            input signals that are independent of
the type of estimation algorithm. Any scalar norm of M can, in
                                                                principle, be used
a a criterion for the optimization of input
                                            signals, however, only the following
four norms are most 'equently mentioned in the literature;

  - min
          Tr(M")   , i.e., minimize the average variance of the parameters,

  - min
          2.,m(M") where /im is the maximum eígenvalue of M,

  - max
          (det(M)) i.e., maximization of the deterrninant of M,

  - max ln
             (det(M)),

and many other equivalent criteria like the maximization of the trace of M.I
addition to the norm optimization some constraints are
                                                             imposed on the
optimization process, in general in the form of a energy limit imposed to the
input. The choice of the optimization criteria is associated with the input
objective. For instance, it may be necessary that some parameters must be
                                                                             very
accurately identied while others may not be so, then such a objective is best
met by using a weighted trace of the infonnation matrix.
                                                             Altematively if it is
desired to maximize the overall
                                identiability of the responses, such a objective



                                         86
may be achieved by using the determinant of the information matrix, a precludes
Gupta et al [59].


Optimal input design was initially developed for linear systems later, however,
the methodology application was extended to non-linear system.
                                                                    Initially the
frequency domain methodology presented some advantage over the time domain
techniques, however, later with the advent of better digital computer and further
development of system identication the time domain technique prevailed.
Frequency domain techniques are in general dependent on the assumption of
system stationarity requiring in this way a system asymptotically stable which
imposes some limits to its applicability to aircra. Otherwise the stationarity
requirement in the frequency domain demands actual input signals in the time
domain of at least several times the largest characteristic period of the
                                                                          system.

In the time domain the optimization of input signals is fonnulated in terms of
non-linear optimal control problem whose solution is most
                                                             comnonly obtained by
using the Maximum Likelihood technique.         A comnon problem to any input
design technique is that the input signals to be generated depend on the values of
the system parameters which nomally are not well known. This
                                                                      generates a
problem of circularity whose solution can be achieved iteratively by using new
calculated values to improve next input and so on.
                                                            Anyway, the problem
demands good initial values of aircraft parameters 'om wind tunnel tests which
sometimes are not available.


The application of optimal input design produces, in
                                                     general, sinusoidal inputs or
other format difficult to be performed by a pilot. I
                                                     general they are much more
applicable to aircra equipped with special instrumentation to reproduce pre-
programned inputs.

 Some researchers and user of system identication for aircraft
                                                                           parameter
estimation, such as Maine and Iliff [40] and Klein [19] concludes that results
almost as good as those obtained 'om optimal
                                                      input design can be obtained
through a careful design of the manoeuvre/input byqfollowing the general rules
discussed above. Mulder [43] comparing optimal and
                                                                  non-optimal inputs
obtained almost similar results from heuristically
                                                    designed doublets and multistep
inputs, thus indicating that a well designed input may compete with a input
obtained from optimization approach. Similar results are
                                                              reported by Plaetschke
et al [44] when the '32l 1' input presented results similar or in some cases
                                                                             superior
to those obtained with optimum inputs
                                            designed using di`erent methods and
optimization criteria.




                                         87
5.2 Jetstream Input Design


For the present research no optimal input has been considered due to the
characteristics of the aircraft, due to what has been said above (Klein, Maine and
Ilifl) and due to the methodology used in the research. Input design in general is
performed in Maximum Likelihood estimation amework while the methodology
used in this work is based on linear regression and Kalman lter
                                                                applications.



For the analysis of the Jetstream phugoid two different
                                                          inputs have been chosen;

 (i) elevator multistep '31 1'.
 (ii) elevator multistep '321 1'.

In principle it is required to excite
                                      simultaneously the short period and the
phugoid in order to identify/extract a much information as possible for the
complete longitudinal model. This imposes some constraints on the time length of
the 'unit' of input, that is, time duration of each
                                                    step component of the surface
displacement. Too short duration, high frequency, excites only the short period
while longer duration input, low frequency, may excite the
                                                                phugoid only. I
principle the Jetstream's short period frequency is around 1.7 rd/s while the
phugoid frequency around 0.15 rd/s is expected for the test conditions (120 Kt
IAS and 5000 f pressure altitude).


Considering a doublet input, to excite the short period a 'unit' step in the range of
 1.0 to 1.2 seconds is considered acceptable. To excite the
                                                                   phugoid a lower
'equency would be required and the doublet being a symetric input probably
would be not adequate. To excite a phugoid a
                                                 step input seems more adequate.
This leads to the input here denominated '311' which is
                                                            composed by one step
input of three 'units' of duration plus a unitary doublet. The step excites the
phugoid while the doublet excites the short period. Therefore, the traditional
'321 1' can not be discarded. The '321 1' is
                                                probably less eective than the '31 1'
because the step '2' tends to cancel out the effect of the
                                                           step '3'.
The unit step of 1.0 to 1.2 seconds can produce a
                                                      step input of reasonable length
to excite the phugoid resulting also in a doublet with the
                                                               required size to excite
the short period.
The model defined for state estimation is non-linear and
                                                             imposes no restriction to
manoeuvre amplitude. Elevator step angles
                                                ranging between 3 and 5 degrees do
not endanger the aircraft and crew and
                                           probably are enough to excite the modes.
The displacement of the elevator
                                         may be used to defme the amount of
perturbation and consequently the phugoid airspeed Variation around the
stabilized initial value (Eg. +/- 10 Kt, +/- 5 Kt).




                                         88
It is assumed that only one input is required. A second
                                                             input during the same
manoeuvre is undesirable because it will disturb the phugoid
                                                                   making it more
difficult to identify the iteration between propeller thrust and
                                                                  phugoid damping
characteristics. After the input the elevator must retum to its initial
                                                                        steady-state
condition and must be held xed until the end of the
                                                          recording period, or may
retum and remain free, depending on which type of
                                                      phugoid is under analysis. In
case of damped phugoid the aircra must be allowed to
                                                            perform more than one
period, being desirable two to three periods.


Figure 5.2 below shows the heuristically designed inputs to the Jetstream test
campaign. The number '1' denotes one unit of input, '2' demotes two units, and '3'
denotes three units.

                                        RESULTS



6.1 Methodology Development and Associated Flight Test
                                                                Program.

The ight test programme performed during the research was associated with the
gradual development of the methodology.
The research comprised six major steps, as follows;


(i)     Development of the Stepwise Regression algorithm and Fortran code.
(ii)    Development of the Extended Kalman Filter (EKF), Iterated Extended
        Kalman Filter (IEKF) algorithms and Fortran codes.
(iii)   Implementation of the Modied Bryson-Frazier smoother associated
        with the EKF.
(iv)    Implementation of the Fixed Lag smoother-differentiator.
(v)     Implementation of the Fixed Lag smoother-differentiator associated
        with the EKF and IEKF and its application for aircra
                                                             parameter estimation.
(vi)    Application of the E-B-M technique to the analysis of the Jetstream
        low speed phugoid.


The Stepwise Regression algorithn and Fortran code were
                                                       initially developed
and tested with data generated by a ACSL (Advanced Continuous Simulation
Language) simulation program developed with the model and data of the B-747
from Heey and Wayne [50]. I a second
                                           step the evaluation of the stepwise
regression has been carried out with ight data. Data 'om the H Jetstream
short period manoeuvres for di`erent inputs such as elevator
                                                             doublet, multistep
'32l 1', and frequency sweep have been used when a evaluation of
                                                                     inputs has
been carried out too. The stepwise regression
                                              presented good results with the
ight data mainly when the data was pre-conditioned by using digital lters.
During this phase some short period simplied models have been estimated for
several manoeuvres. Simulations carried out with the estimated short
                                                                          period
models reproduced very well the aircra manoeuvres. It has been observed that
any of the three tested inputs excited very well the short period mode, however,
the short period frequency calculated 'om
                                              equency sweep input had some
tendency to reect the predominant 'equency of the sweep which was in general
close to the short period 'equency.


The development of the EKF algoritlnn and its
                                          implementation a a Fortran code
has been perfomed with data 'om an ACSL simulation
                                                   program of the Jetstream




                                         90
aircraft obtained 'om Desrochers [22]. I a rst step noiseless data was used. I
a second step noise generated by a ACSL intrinsic function was added to the
three accelerations. The algorithm showed robustness and encouraged the use of
ight data. Further developments of the EKF algorithm with ight data has been
carried out with data from an EMB-312 Tucano Trainer stall and Dutch Roll
ight, supplied by Embraer. An altemative EKF algorithm has been implemented
in this phase when short period models were estimated inside the EKF for the
available Jetstream ight data using the scheme forinulated
                                                                 by Bauer and
Andrisani [30] when the lter estimated models very similar to those obtained
                                                                             by
the stepwise regression. Appendix E shows the results for the
                                                                      Stepwise
Regression and EKF for one short period test point.                     i

To the EKF has been initially added the Modied Bryson-Frazier smoother. This
smoother proved effective for ight data analysis, however it demonstrated
                                                                          high
demand for computer temporary storage and processing time. This
                                                                  development
phase has been carried out with the Tucano data used in the development of the
EKF.


The analysis of the performance of the Gauss-Markov temis used to model the
three aerodynamic force components and three moments in the EKF led to the
attempt to estimate some aerodynamic derivatives from the analysis of paitial
derivatives modelled as second order Gauss-Markov process inside the EKF.
That is, for expressions like the following;

                                  -       Øf     Øfåt
                                                  1

       7 is known and X ,í would be represented as Gauss-Markov process.
This representation is possible, however it is
                                              highly dependent on the process
noise dened intemally for every term of the Gauss-Markov models.


The search for a criterion to dene the process noise and the search for a
efñcient algorithm to differentiate ight data led to the Fixed
                                                                  Lag smoother-
dierentiator a proposed by Fioretti e Jetto [34]. The
                                                               development and
evaluation of the Fixed Lag smoother dierentiator has been carried out with the
Jetstream short period and the Tucano data. The
                                                 process noise as dened in the
work of Fioretti and Jetto [34] is applicable to a
                                                    single variable, thus it was
inadequate for the requirements of equation 6.1 which is multivariable. Attempts
have been carried out using measurement and
                                                process noise calculated by the



                                            91
Fixed Lag smoother in single parameters estimated in the EKF and that were also
measured. Some results were satisfactory for noisy parameters
                                                                 denoting that this
is a way to ty in follow on research.


The requirement of a differentiator in aircra parameter estimation and the
efciency of the Fixed Lag smoother led to extend its use as an altemative
smoother to the MBF smoother. The development of the Fixed
                                                           Lag smoother and
a comparative analysis of its performance against the MBF smoother has been
carried out with the Tucano Dutch Roll data.


Having developed the algorithms and computer codes the remaining ights were
devoted to the case study, the analysis of the Jetstream
                                                         phugoid, which demanded
4 test points to dene the input length required to excite
                                                           simultaneously the short
period and the phugoid and 6 test points to produce 4 valid phugoids. I addition
6 test points have been carried out to
                                         identify the propulsive efficiency of the
Jetstream in ight conditions close to that expected for the
                                                               phugoid. The tests
have been carried out in the Jetstream-100 G-AXUI in the Craneld area and the
Craneld Airñeld has been used as base. Attempts have been made to avoid
ight under high turbulence, however, in general light turbulence was always
observed during tests.
Computer codes developed during the research are documented in reference [60].



6.2 Preliminary Data Analysis


Figure 6.1 below shows the general procedure for parameter estimation in the
Estimation-Before-Modelling technique.

Before a data set can be used for parameter estimation several data checks have
to be performed.    One of the rst data check/analysis consists in to identify the
continuity of the data set. At the same time it is observed whether the data
contains or not spikes and drop outs which
                                           may jeopardize the performance of the
Kalman lter. Spikes and drop outs have to be removed
                                                         by any editing process,
manual or automatic. I this research only manual edition has been
                                                                    perfonned by
using the edit option of the graphic soware package Egraph.




                                        92
                                                   Extemal
