       Figure 6.1 General procedure for parameter estimation using the EBM
                       technique.

Aer this preliminary check the data must be checked for
                                                            consistency in units
when conversion to a common system of units
                                                    compatible with the model
equations is performed. At this point the airdata parameters must be calculated
when from the calculation of air density ratio
                                               parameters such a true airspeed,
Mach number, dynamic pressure are calculated.
                                                   Analogously aircra weight,
inertias and cg position are calculated from the aircra
                                                         loading fomi and fuel
consumption during ight and added to the data set. The calculation of the



                                                93
propeller thrust must also be perfonned using the propeller charts or the
propulsive efciency determined in ight by following the procedure described in
Chapter 2. The results must be included in the common data le. The propulsive
efciency tests results are discussed in section 6.6.5.

The algoritlnns for state estimation and model identication operate on data sets
consisting of sets of measurements pertaining each set to one instant of time.
Since the instrumentation system does not allow to collect a complete data set at
the same instant of time because in fact it scans a set of parameters before
recording, then the parameters are not obtained at the same instant of time. I
other words, there are time shifts between parameters of a set which for the
instrumentation system are recorded at a common reference time instant. Iliff and
Maine [40] recommend that time shifcs larger than 10 ms must be taken into
account for parameters like accelerations and body rates while for parameters like
airspeed, altitude, etc., time shifts much larger than 10 ms are not signicant. A
second source of time shis is the time delays due to transmission or calculation
of parameters inside systems like the Inertial Reference System
                                                                   (IRS), Air Data
Computer, etc. The analysis of the Jetstream instrumentation revealed that special
attention must be given to the three body rates, three acceleration
                                                                        components
and attitude pitch and roll which are calculated
                                                         intemally by the Inertial
Reference System and that have an appreciable Transport Delay, as dened and
presented in reference [51]. Thus, to take into account the time shis, dened
their values for each parameter, interpolation must be
                                                         performed in the recorded
data in order to bring all data to a comnon reference. I general the
                                                                      input (control
surface displacement) is taken as reference.


An analysis of time shis carried out early in the research
                                                              programme based on
the analysis of actual ight data showed that for
                                                sampling rates larger than 20
samples per second, for a aircra of the class of Jetstream, time shifts due to
instrumentation scanning have no special meaning at the end of the linear
regression process. It is specially valid for a manoeuvre like the phugoid that
does not involve large accelerations or body rates, the main case
                                                                   study of this
research. Regarding data sampling, for the Jetstream the data has been recorded
at 50 samples per second, however it has been
                                                processed at 25 samples per
second to reduce processing time. It has been observed that this reduction in
sampling did not introduce signicant errors to the overall process. A description
of the Jetstream instrumentation system is
                                              presented in Williams [52] while
perfonnance characteristics of the main sensors are presented in references [5l],
[53] and [58].
With reference to the Tucano data no time shi correction was considered due to
lack of infonnation, however the data was recorded with 32
                                                            samples per second.




                                        94
  6.3 State Estimation


  Three different EKF algorithm
                                   congurations have been implemented as Fortran
 codes;


 (i)   Ordinary EKF algorithm comprising equations 3.14 to 3.23

 (ii) Iterated Extended Kalman Filter comprising
                                                     equations 3.24 to 3.32

 (iii) Simplied Iterated Extended comprising equations 3.14 to 3.23 with
                                                                               3.20
       replaced by 3.31.

 The algorithms were transforned to
                                       computer codes by following the equations in
  didactic form and easy to understand and
                                             modify. Numerical integration for state
  propagation was implemented by using a fourth order
                                                            Runge-Kutta integration
  scheme. Covariance time
                            propagation was implemented by using the state model
  gradient function, equation 3.19, due to the absence of a true transition
                                                                              matrix.
  Comparisons have been carried out with this model and the
                                                                     propagation via
  numerical integration of the Riccati
                                        equation by a Runge-Kutta scheme and by
 assuming that F (equation 3.16) remains constant over the
                                                                propagation ínterval,
 as assumed by Bauer and Andrisani
                                       [30], and results very similar were obtained.
 Double precision variables and arithmetics have
                                                     been used in the integrations,
 matrix inversions and covariance
                                   updates in order to avoid numerical degradation
 due to roundoff problems. Final
                                    algorithm confgurations were very similar to
 those described by Stengel and
                                 Sri-Jayantha [8].

  To the rst algorithm
                            conguration (i) the Modied-Bryson-Frazier
  smoother was added.                                                            (MBF)
                              Initial trials with the Fixed
                                                                 Lag (FL) smoother-
  differentiator have been carried out associated with
                                                          the ordinary EKF algorithm
 dened above as rst
                          conguration with the MBF smoother replaced
                                                                              by the FL
 smoother. Since the rst trials it became evident
                                                         that the MBF could correct
 (remove bias) the states calculated by the EKF and whose
                                                                  estimates resulted in
 high covariance values. The FL smoother however showed that
                                                                        it behaves as a
 digital lter only reducing scatter and has no
                                                    power to remove tendencies from
the estimates
                relying then on the ñlter calculations. The Fixed
                                                                    Lag smoother uses
 information 'om 'lag' samples whilst the MBF
                                                   smoother uses information 'om all
the past samples because it is a xed
                                              ínterval smoother. Thus it has been
observed the necessity to ty to reduce the
                                                inherent bias of the EKF when
the FL smoother. The rst                                                          using
                               attempt in this direction was to use the FL with the
complete IEKF. Tests with this lter conguration resulted in a
                                                                        very high time
demanding algorithm, however with apparent high
                                                       quality results. A altemative
solution has been implemented
                                   by combining the FL with the Simplied Iterated




                                         95
Extended Kalman lter, a described by Moeck [31], where the iteration is
performed in the measurement terms of the lter only. This lter conguration
has been adopted to be used with the FL smoother.


The use of methodology based on Kalman ltering and
                                                          smoothing requires a
prjiori specication of the process and measurement noise covariance matrices.
Modem instrumentation produces measurements that for actual use
                                                                        may be
considered as completely independent. This means that the measurement errors
of different variables may be assumed to be uncorrelated. This allows to assume
that the covariance matrices of the process and measurement noise can be
considered as diagonal, Mulder [43].         I principle the covariance of the
measurement errors could be estimated from the residuals of
                                                                          laboratory
calibrations. Therefore, when installed in the aircraft the sensors are
                                                                        exposed to a
series of additional errors from several sources such as structural
                                                                     Vibration, noise
'om electrical power sources, etc. I this research the measurement noise
covariances have been determined by analysing the time histories of the recorded
state variables in steady-state ight performed before
                                                         every manoeuvre. By
following a procedure described by Bauer and Andrisani [3O], a noise envelope
was dened for each variable by effectively
                                           drawing boundaries representing ±1o-
(1 standard deviation) about the local mean of the variable. The elements of the
measurement noise covariance matrix were then
                                                  given by R. =       Bauer and
Andrisani concludes that this approach is valid when the
                                                            process noise is kept
low.


The computational technique also requires
                                          good initial estimates for the states
estimated in the process. For the measured state variables initial values were
obtained 'om trim data recorded immediately
                                            prior to the commencement of the
dynamic manoeuvre, i.e., prior to the input. For umneasured variables a initial
value must be specied and during the lter
                                               tuning process it must be observed
whether these initial values affect, or not, the lter nal estimates. When the
ight immediately prior to the manoeuvre of interest is a out of trim dynamic
condition, good starting values can be estimated by the algorithn dened in
                                                                              (ii)
of section 6.1 which includes a ñxed
                                         point smoother. I general after twenty
samples the estimated initial values are good and the respective covariances too.
Then the process may be aborted and the EKF
                                                 algorithm may be restarted with
updated initial values of states.

Initial values of the covariance matrix, assumed to be
                                                         diagonal, needed to be
dened too. For the states that are measured the covariances of the initial
                                                                              state
values were assumed to be equal to the measurement noise dened for
                                                                         the same
states. Bauer and Andrisani [30] also assume that for non-measured
                                                                        states the
initial covariances may be made equal to one fourth of a initial value
                                                                       dened for



                                        96
the state, i.e., it may be assumed
                                     I, =(%x.)2.
                                              Again the IEKF with xed point
smoother may be used to estimate covariances of the initial state values.
                                                                          Anyway
if no possibility exits to dene the initial covariances,
                                                            large values must be
attributed, as recommended in several sources, however the inuence of such
values over the lter nal estimates must be analysed during the
                                                                 tuning process.

The process noise covariance matrix Q, assumed
                                                     diagonal, is difficult to specify.
The evaluation of its elements is a iterative process, referred as
                                                                     tuning, in which
the main objective is to minimise the lter residual
                                                            sequences ensuring the
convergence of the lter gain and covariance matrices. It is diicult to dene
initial values. Without acceptable initial values it is sometimes
                                                                   impossible to start
up the lter. It has been observed along the research that the initial values of the
process noise may be assumed to be 10 to 1000 times the respective measurement
noise. For large initial values of process noise the lter
                                                           simply reproduces the
measurements or eventually diverges. Reducing the process noise values, the
estimates become more 'ltered' and continuing to reduce the
                                                                 process noise the
process starts to produce high 'equency noise and eventually diverges. The best
choice of initial values for the elements of Q
                                               corresponds to a point just before
the onset of the high frequency noise in the output. From this
                                                                 point ne tuning
only is required to achieve good lter performance. Note that the onset is
specic for each state. Anyway, it is not difcult to tune measured states whose
measurements are considered as accurate. I these cases there is no reason for the
lter to produce estimates completely different om the measurements.
                                                                           Tuning
rst the measured states the overall tuning becomes easier. It is
                                                                 only necessary to
rely on the lter tuning to dene good process noise for those variables of
dubious accuracy or completely unknown. The
                                                    tuning process requires also
engineering judgment because the residuals may be minimized by following
closely the measurements, however, it is not the objective of tuning since the
noise has to be removed 'om the estimate. A similar
                                                       tuning process is also used
to reñne the initial estimates for the elements of the state covariance matrix P
(diagonal) in order to minimise their inuence on the nal estimates. The same is
also applicable to the states inital values.
With care it is assured that the nal estimates are a valid as the available
measured data allows to have.


 6.4 Data Processing - State Estímatíon


The results presented here are based on the
                                            analysis of a EMB-312 Tucano
Trainer Dutch Roll manoeuvre. The data was collected
                                                     (with 32 samples/sec.) on
a opportunity basis rather than in dedicated
                                               ight for parameter estimation
purpose. Thus, no particular care has been taken to set up the manouvre
appropriately as no adequate control input for parameter estimation was



                                         97
performed. However, the mode was excited. Despite these deciencies the data
is very useful because it allows to identify a lot of characteristics in the
identication process that would be imperceptible if the manouevre had been
performed with the care required for parameter estimation.

The same data, i.e., the Dutch Roll manoeuvre, was analysed on two different
occasions. On the rst occasion it was very difficult to do the EKF to
                                                                      converge
and to dene a acceptable tuning to the lter. The problem became more and
more accentuated as additional bias terms were introduced to the model.         After
setting up different models with three bias terms only per model it was observed
that the most signicant terms were those due to the
                                                              airspeed, sideslip and
incidence. Then it was decided to analyse the data with these three bias terms
only.
On the second occasion the data was analysed it was decided to formulate a
model with at least six bias terms plus a scale factor, a follows;

  -
    airspeed bias
  - incidence bias
  -
    sideslip bias
  - rate of roll bias
  - rate of
            yaw bias
  - lateral acceleration bias.
  - incidence scale factor


The three rst terms are the most possible to exist because the measurement
variables are more susceptible to measurement errors.
                                                         Otherwise, the whole set
correspond to a set more adequate for the lateral mode analysis. The analysis of a
model with the above seven proposed terms revealed a
                                                           initially divergent EKF
and demanded a lot of attempts to dene two sets of noise
                                                             parameters dening a
range inside which the EKF lter became convergent. Inside this range it was
also possible to do more ne tuning of the lter.
                                                   Surprisingly the MBF smoother
diverged for several parameters in the sample range for which the EKF
covariance values were not yet stabilised. A analysis of the
                                                               possible divergence
causes revealed that it could be caused mainly by the terms,


                        lH1<PK/K-g '' R]   and   lH<P</K-Hl< '' RT]      (6-2)

generated by the EKF and transferred to the MBF smoother. These terms a`ect
the gain and the covariance update in the lter and the
                                                       adjoint variables in the
MBF smoother which are also highly dependent on the lter covariance matrix
and Kalman gain a well. It has been observed that both the EKF and the
                                                                          MBF
smoother were extremely dependent on whether
                                             single or double precision



                                           98
arithmetics were used in the calculations. I a ñrst attempt to solve the
                                                                         problem
all variables not yet fornulated as double precision were transfonned to double
precision, the covariance matrix was symetrized aer every update and a new
inversion process based on L-U decomposition was used. This
                                                               approach did not
change the results. A second attempt to solve the problem consisted of replacing
the expression for the covariance update given by,


                              PK/K = (I` KKHK/K-1 )PK/K-1               (6-3)

by the stabilised covariance update given


               by:PK/K
                = (I " KKHK/K-i )PK/K- (I _ KKHK/K- )T + KKRKQ         (6-4)

 This expression is considered more stable than equation 6.3, less
                                                                     susceptible to
roundoff problems associated with Kalman covariance
                                                         update, a concluded by
Gelb [29] and other authors. The new expression
                                                     required a new lter tuning
because the lter became divergent with the noise
                                                       settings obtained with the
covariance updated by equation 6.3. Again the MBF smoother was
                                                                         divergent
which evidenced the requirement for improvements in the covariance matrix
update. A third attempt to improve the results was performed by transfoming the
EKF lter algorithm in a U-D update algorithm as dened
                                                              by Bierman [39] and
summarized in Appendix C. This algorithm is considered more efñcient and less
susceptible to roundoff than the ordinary formulation and has the main feature of
not requiring matrix inversíon. The new algorithm results led to a
                                                                    divergent EKF
using the timing values of the previous confgurations thus requiring a new
tuning.    Since the U-D algorithm does not calculate directly terms like
[HPHT +R] or its inverse then it is not compatible with the MBF smoother. By
generating the required terms attempts were made to identify the perfonnance of
the MBF smoother with the new values of state and
                                                      covariance estimated by the
U-D algorithm resulting again in MBF smoother
                                                 divergence.                _
A analysis of matrix [HIPHT +R] based on its
                                                singular values revealed that it is
near singular or rank decient and
                                      consequently some system states are near
observable only. It is important to note that the MBF smoother
                                                                  diverged in the
region where the covariance and Kalman gain have not yet reached steady-state
conditions. It seems that the MBF smoother is less stable than the associated
EKF because despite the apparently critical conditions the EKF
                                                                  converges for a
set of process noise and in a range around these
                                                  values, however, the associated
MBF smoother diverges.
Figure 6.2.a presents the aileron and rudder inputs of the manoeuvre. Figures
6.2.b, c and d, below, obtained 'om EKF-MBF algorithm with stabilised
covariance update show, (a) the lateral speed
                                              component v, (b) the roll rate p and
(c) the yaw rate r 'om the EKF and the MBF smoother plotted together.

As stated by Bierman [39] a small element in the
                                                       diagonal matrix D is a clear
 indication of near singularity which for the state estimation
                                                                problem is analogous
to near unobservability of some states. However, there are no indications of how
 small the element should be to reect the ill
                                              conditioning. For the matrix D above
the smallest element is D(7,7) which is associated with the attitude
                                                                           pitch (0).
However, D(7,7) is not so small to be associated with the instability of the
lter/smoother. The analysis of q and 9 estimates and the
                                                              respective covariances
showed no evidences of major estimation
                                            difculty. It seems that this criterion is
not suicient to identify the causes of the identication
                                                           problem. The analysis of
the singular values of the matrix [HPHT +R] recorded in the
                                                                  divergent region of
the MBF smoother revealed several
                                        singular values very small denoting thus
associated rank deciency of the matrix. Similar
                                                        analysis performed with the



                                                        104
covariance matrix revealed also the same problem.                                                                                   I both cases the condition
indices of several singular values, a dened in
                                                 Chapter 4, resulted in very large
numbers which may be considered as indications of associated rank
                                                                     deciency of
the matrices. For the matrix [HPHT +R] the
                                             biggest calculated condition number
was 8.75E+07 while for the covariance matrix a number of the same order was
calculated too. An analysis of the correlation matrix on the
                                                                 divergent region
revealed some correlation between the bias terms and their
                                                                        respective
measurements. It has been rther observed that these correlations decresed as
more samples were being processed, however some
                                                     signicant correlations were
retained at the end of the process for the
                                           airspeed and sideslip bias terms. No
correlation was observed between other states (eg, 0).

estimates are stable independent of initial values and
                                                            change only their

                                                     region around the tuning
condition.

Figure 6.5 a, b and c show some bias terms. Figure 6.5.a corresponds to the bias
in airspeed which is convergent, almost
                                          independent of initial values and valid for
a very small value of process noise (it is a random
                                                     constant). Figure 6.5.b shows
the incidence bias. This tenn never presented a well dened
                                                                     convergence as
would be expected for a well conditioned estimate.
                                                      However, it is very small and
held this characteristics in all attempted
                                             tuning. The scale factor to incidence
measurement also never presented a convergent result and was
                                                                   always dependent
on the initial covariance value and had to be removed from the model
                                                                             (lateral
manoeuvre with small incidence excursion). Figure 6.5.c shows the
                                                                        sideslip bias
which is convergent and with all characteristics of a well conditioned estimate.
Figure 6.6 a, b and c show residuals due to airspeed, sideslip and incidence,
respectively. The airspeed and the sideslip residuals show no shis and are very
small and with all characteristics of well
                                           conditioning and whiteness, etc. Figure

not identified a a mismodelling problem.

 tenns were nearly observable only. They were the main reason for the

This section showed results not
                                    satisfactory a would be expected for the
methodology involved. Normally estimation process like the Extended Kalman
Filter are seen or are presented as almost
                                             miraculous, i.e., allows to determine
unknown terms in very complex models. I
                                              practical applications, however, the
user has no control over the whole
                                    process and the assumptions over which the
lter is based are no longer valid. This is the case of the
                                                             measurement noise of
the model used above which was
                                  probably one of the reasons for lack of success
in the detemination of all desired terms. I fact some
                                                          measurements had very
low resolution. For the procedure used to determine the
                                                               measurement noise
covariance a measurement with low resolution was seen a a
                                                                  measurement with
very low noise or very high accuracy, what is not true. Thus, the low
measurement noise covariance indirectly
                                           inply low process noise covariance and
 in general very small terms are added to the covariances at
                                                               every update leading
to a set of very small numbers that cause
                                          problems at some stage of the estimation
process. Otherwise the lter was infonned that high accuracy measurements were
available while in fact low accuracy were
                                            effectively available. I fact the model
and the methodology can produce
                                     good results a can be seen in references [8]
and [18]. For the present case, it would be
                                             preferable to use sensor error statistics
from laboratory calibration rather than the
                                                 approach used, at least for the
parameters with low resolution, however no data were available. I fact
                                                                                good



                                                                          113
results with Kalman ltering starts in the laboratory with good models for the
measurement errors, which unfortunately was not the case in the present research.



6.5 Comparative Analysis Between the Modiñed Bryson-Frazier Smoother
and the Fixed Lag Smoother Congurations.


The analysis of the Dutch Roll data showed divergence of the MBF smoother
algorithm as presented the previous section. It has been observed, however, that
for a simplified measurement model, i.e., a model with bias tenrs in airspeed,
sideslip and incidence only, the MBF smoother did not diverge. Therefore, this
configuration was chosen for a comparative analysis between the two smoothing
techniques.

With the simplied model the Estimation-Before-Modelling technique was
applied to the Dutch Roll manoeuvre in order to identify a lateral model for the
aircra. A total of 31 states were estimated including the Gauss-Markov terms
and the three bias terms. Scalíng factors for incidence and sideslip resulted in
small and unstable terms and were discarded based on the results of the
                                                                        previous
section. Otherwise the Fortran program in general was very close to limiting
                                                                           by
the PC memory size or by intrinsic Fortran limits such a Allocatable matrices
bigger than 64K bytes. In general during the analysis of the previous section the
Fortran compiler presented warning regarding program size and
                                                                  impossibility of
optimization when generating the program EXEC module.

The Fixed Lag smoother was initially congured with the HEKF and later with the
simplified version of the IEKF, as presented in chapter 4, because the rst
conguration demanded so much processing time. With reference to the EKF-
MBF and IEKF-FL, both algorithms produced very acceptable results with both
gain and covariance converging fast to good and stable values. For the Dutch
Roll analysed both algorithns estimated large bias for the
                                                             sideslip angle and for
the airspeed. It was observed some inconsistency between the lateral acceleration
and the sideslip angle resulting in poor estimates of the related states.
                                                                          Examples
of the performance of the two state estimation algorithm are shown in
                                                                            gures
6.12, 6.13 and 6.14, below. The gures showed comparisons of the nomalised
lateral force X, the nornalised rolling moment L and the normalised
                                                                            yawing
moment N1, respectively, a calculated by the two algorithms.

             Figure 6.14                       Nomalised moment N1 from EKF-MBF and from IEKF-FL




The estimates are very similar and the main differences are due to the smoothers.
The MBF smoother modies the estimates by removing bias whilst the simpler
Fixed Lag smoother behaves as a digital lter by reducing scatter in the estimated
data. It has been observed that this difference is more accentuated in variables
calculated with low condence, i.e., it resulted in high covariances at the end of
the EKF calculation process. This accentuates the action of the MBF resulting in
larger differences between the estimates of the two algorithms. The estimates of
other states such a p, r, ø, etc., are almost equal to the ight test measurements,
independent of the smoother used. The main dierences in the whole process are
those shown for the normalised moments L and N1, above.


For the example a lag time constant equivalent to 15 samples (or 15 iteration
steps) was used. This value has been obtained by monitoring the dominant
eigenvalue of the Kalman lter dynamic equation (equation 3.63). The time
constant varied between 5 and ll integration steps depending of the variable in
analysis, however only one lag value was used for all variables and was based on
three times the smallest observed time constant.      It has been observed that
increasing the smoother lag shifts the derivative to the le, i.e., anticipates the
derivative related to the original signal. This is also true for the smoothed states,
however at very low scale. With reference to the example, the FL smoother data
model was based on the data signal to be smoothed more three derivatives which




                                                                                         116
was considered a compromise between results quality and processing time.
Therefore, a minimum of two derivatives are required to reproduce satisfactorily
the signal because the derivative of high order is noise only.


The same process noise (tuning) was used in both lter algorithms and seems to
be acceptable because in terms of lters both algorithms are almost
                                                                                 equal.
However, dierent tuning may be used and this may contribute to reduce or the
accentuate the differences in the results. It was observed that it is difficult to tune
the states modelled as Gauss-Markov process like L and
                                                                  N1, for which the
measurement model has no direct residual to be analysed. At some instance the
tuning objective of the EKF seems to be opposed by the tuning objective of the
MBF smoother. For example, if the process noise is reduced to result in less
noisy estimates this increases the action of the MFB smoother by modifying
completely its smoothed estimates relative to the EKF values. Actually, the
reduction in the process noise changed the shape of the EKF estimates while the
MBF modied more strongly its estimates trying to bring them back to their true
(unique) values. In fact the MBF smoother estimates are more repeatable and less
dependent on the process noise. This is not true for the Fixed Lag smoother that
behaves more as a digital lter and the smoothed estimates depend
                                                                  directly on the
IEKF estimates and the process noise used in their detennination. For the states
modelled as second order Gauss-Markov process it is important to have
                                                                             enough
process noise distributed in all the three terms of the model in order to allow it to
represent better the state it is modelling. When there is enough process noise the
MBF smoother estimates tend to be equal to the EKF estimates and the IEKF-FL
estimates. Excess of process noise only increases the noise in the estimates,
however not modifying signicantly the smoothed estimates.


The MBF is not so powerful a a digital lter. I the case that the lter retums
noisy estimates the MBF smoother does not produces clean estimates while the
Fixed Lag smoother does.          The performance of the Fixed Lag smoother-
differentiator algorithm a a dierentiator is illustrated by
                                                            gures 6.15 and 6.16.
The gures show the lateral Velocity component v and the nonnal
                                                                           Velocity
component w and their respective derivatives estimated for the Dutch Roll
manoeuvre for a lag of 15 iteration steps. The derivative estimates are considered
to be accurately representative since there is no
                                                  appreciable phase shift between
the state variables and their derivatives.

                                                                                              118
The calculated process noise based on the variance matching technique
                                                                          proposed
by Fioretti and Jetto [34], results in a value that in general is larger than what
would be expected for a process noise covariance value, mainly for those
parameters that have a large signal to noise ratio. For noisy parameters the
calculated values of process noise are more in line with the expected values.
These large values may be justied by the objective of the smoother that is to
produce the derivatives whilst simultaneously reproducing the state variables as
Taylor series whose terms comprises the calculated derivatives. A large process
noise is thus required by the lter to follow as closely as possible the data
                                                                             signal,
which is the measurement for the lter.


The use of the Fixed Lag smoother-differentiator requires initial estimates of the
process and measurement noise to be used in a process where the optimal values
of these two parameters will be determined for each state to be smoothed.
Provided that good initial estimates are given to the algorithm then the time
required by the Fixed Lag smoother to compute a set of smoothed variables and
their derivatives is less than the time required by the MBF smoother. The initial
estimates in general constitute no problem because once a rst set is detennined
for the rst tuning attempt then any other value will be close to this rst set. For
ne tuning it is not required to compute new process and measurement noise
covariance estimates.
For the model used in the example the EKF-MBF
                                                   algorithm required 10 Mbytes
of computer temporary storage while the IEKF-FL
                                                  required 200 Kbytes.

The aerodynamic model of the Tucano,
                                           comprising the stability and control
derivatives to represent appropriately the Dutch Roll manoeuvre was estimated
using the regression procedure described in Chapter 4 with the estimated state
variables as input. For each of the evaluated state estimation
                                                                procedure, i.e., the
EKF with MBF smoother and the IEKF with Fixed
                                                     Lag smoother, time histories
of the estimated states were stored in tabular format files for
                                                                easy access by the
regression procedure. The estimated variables stored in this way comprised the
normalised sideforce K, normalised roll and yaw moments
                                                            L N, and the states v,
p, r, Ø, , w, W, ii, v-V, (v-7)2, w-W and (w-W)2, with V and W denoting
average values. The derivatives of the MBF estimated variables were calculated         1
by using the Fixed Lag smoother-differentiator.

The regressions were performed by trying to
                                               identify models represented by the
general fomi;




                                        119



                                                                                       I
    1                                                                       (6.5)
         0     _ 0    o      o      0         0   o       o       o     o



where the coefñcients of the equations are the dimensional
                                                                stability and control
derivatives.


Regressions were performed for each of the independent variables X, L and N,
using rst, the stored state variables estimated by the EKF-MBF algorithm and
second, the stored state. variables estimated by the IEKF-FL algorithm. Data were
processed with data records as stored in the les and with the same data centered
and scaled as described by Draper and Smith [36] and no differences were
observed.     Regressions were not limited to the general model above. In fact
simplied version of the model have been tried as well as more complex versions
were attempted too in order to identify models that would better
                                                                     represent the
manoeuvre. I general the squared terms have been rejected and no di`erences
have been observed in using v and w or their
                                                respective counterparts v-V and
 w-W. It has been observed that, in general, any model
                                                         including both ø and ¼   Î
but omitting p and r had better tting than models with
                                                           p and r and ø and Î    ¼
omitted. However the inclusion of p to models with ø and
                                                                       resulted in
considerable tting improvements.
Note that ø and        were included in the models by statistical reasons, i.e., to
improve the models t. However, lateral models normally do not contain such a
terms (it is physically unrealisable).


When the ight test manoeuvre was performed it was
                                                          certainly not expected that
the recorded data would subsequently be used in
                                                      parameter estimation analysis.
Thus no particular care was taken in setting up the manoeuvre
                                                                 appropriately for
parameter estimation purposes. Although the input excited appropriately the
mode, its was not very suitable for parameter estimation since some correlation
was observed between recorded aileron and rudder
                                                   signal data. Both aileron and
rudder commands were made
                                   simultaneously (see gure 6.2.a) thereby
introducing some collinearity into the estimation process. Depending on the
model conguration the derivative due to the aileron assumed
                                                                       completely
different (and large) values which even changed the
                                                     signs in a clear symptom of
collinearity. This was the case of models without ø and . It has been observed
that adding p to models with ø and    produced more stable values for the aileron
derivatives and improved the t significantly.       However the yaw rate was
frequently rejected 'om the models and was excluded om the best models. The
collinearity problems were solved by choosing more appropriate models and the




                                        120
nal models presented in tables 6.1 to 6.3 were re-processed using the
                                                                      Ridge
Regression procedure as described by Draper and Smith [36] with no result
changes

insignicant, they are also omitted from the tables.

signs conventions.

An analysis of the correlation matrix referent to the EKF-MBF
                                                                                       estimates, that is
common to all MBF models of tables 6.1 to 6.3 (models with
                                                                                 ø and ), showed
no large tenns that would indicate high correlation or
                                                                collinearity. The
eigenvalue analysis of the correlation matrix resulted in the following values for
the minimum and maximum eigenvalues;


         am = o.o492s4sss227419                   ,            am    = 4.1s73oss216442s

resulting in the following condition number,


                                       C = 84.4044

This number is below 1000 which in section 4.9 was considered a threshold for
the indication of collinearity. Thus, from the point of view of condition number
there is no indication of collinearity.
Analogously the singular value analysis of the data matrix, common to all models
of a same algorithm, resulted in the following values for the maximum and
minimum singular values;


                 m = 0. 09969065                       m   = 2.03894



resulting in the following condition index;

                                    Q = 20.45268

Since the condition index is out of the interval [30          100] then there is no
indication of collinearity (as stated in Chapter 4).


Similarly, singular values and eigenvalues were calculated respectively for the
data matrix and correlation matrix of the models without ø and g¼ (EKF-MBF

were obtained;

                                          C = 12.405

                                          C = 7.576


Again there are no indication of collinearity based on the analysis of the condition
number and condition index and it seems that the data matrix is better
conditioned than for the previous model.      Apparently the large increases in the
control derivatives of the sideforce are not caused by
                                                       collinearity.

For the models calculated by using estimates from the IEKF with Fixed
                                                                           Lag
smoother, very similar numbers were calculated. Anyvvay the regressions showed
very sensitive to the aileron, roll rate and attitude roll. It seems that
mathematically it was difficult to identify what is causing rolling and what is
causing yawing due to the superimposed inputs to aileron and rudder. It is
important to note that the aircra was equipped with a automatic rudder trim
however the tab displacement was not recorded in ight nor included to the
models. This was another cause of the data analysis problems.

The correlation coefcients of the models with ø and              returned by the

nonnalised yawing moment 0.98 was calculated for the MBF data and 0.92 for

numbers. For the models without ø and        tenns the con'elation coefcients were
small than the values presented above (e. g., 0.88 for the lateral force model).

 The results presented this section showed that
                                                                                                                    the new smoother may be used as
 a altemative to the MBF
                           mainly when state derivatives are required. It is faster,
requires less computer storage, is stable and is an excellent
                                                               differentiator. The
solution adopted for the bias terms were to correct
                                                    the measurements by the nal
(convergent) estimated values and reprocessing the data in a lter without bias in
the models.



6.6 The Jetstream's
                                               Phugoid Case Study.

6.6.1 General Concepts


The HP-l37A Jetstream-100 is
                                  equipped with two Turbomeca Astazou engines
 driving Hamilton Standard 23LF 3-blade propellers
                                                        hydraulically operated. The
 engine and its propeller are integrated to form a
                                                     single unit that operates under
 pilot control and is safeguarded by the engine
                                                 goveming system. Constant engine
RPM is maintained by means of a
                                       centrifugal govemor that controls a needle
valve metering the fuel. The
                                 pilot sets propeller pitch through a normal servo
system comprising of a hydraulic valve that
                                                 govems the blade angle and also
provides mechanical feed-back to the system. When the
                                                             pilot sets a blade angle
there is a change in
                       torque demand that is sensed by the system which in tum
changes the fuel ow in order to not allow the
                                                   engine RPM to change. Torque




                                                                                          126
changes due to changes in airspeed or due to changes in ight path are sensed too
and the fuel ow responds analogously as it does under pilot control. For
instance, if the aircra is put into a climb, airspeed will decrease the propeller
torque will increase and more fuel will be fed in order to maintain RPM so that
the engine power will increase. In the same way when the aircra is diving the
fuel ow is reduced in order to keep constant the RPM.            The system
characteristics are such that under manoeuvre the aircra speed is held nearly
constant when compared with aircraft equipped with ordinary power control
system. This characteristic is considered an advantage from the operational point
of view since it reduces pilot workload, as stated by the engine and the aircraft
manufacturers. With these characteristics of the power control system it is natural
that during the phugoid large Variations of power may occur and
                                                                      consequently its
iterference with the aircraft longitudinal stability characteristics.
The objective of the study that follows is to identify how this iterference takes
place and whether it contributes or not to the aircraft phugoid characteristics by
analysing the aerodynamic derivatives calculated during a low speed phugoid
with controls free.


The effects of power from propeller operation are generally
                                                                important on the
stability and control characteristics of aircra.    The power affects the lift, the
pitch moment and the drag.


(1) Li: - The effects of a rumiing propeller on the lift forces may be divided in
two groups, those due to the propeller forces and those due to the
                                                                           propeller
slipstream. In the rst group is included the li component of the propeller thrust
vector and the lift due to the propeller nonnal force. The
                                                             slipstream effects are
due to: (i) the changes in lift due to the power induced
                                                               change in dynamic
pressure over the section of wing immersed in the slipstream. (ii) The change in
lift caused by the change in angle of incidence of the immersed
                                                                     portion of the
wing due to the propeller downwash. Analogous slipstream effects are observed
over the horizontal stabilizer.


(2) Pitch Moment: - A propulsive system provides two different sources of pitch
moments: (i) that coming from the forces acting on the unit itself, and
                                                                            (ii) that
coming from the interation of the propulsive slipstream with other parts of the
aircraft. For a propulsive system with propeller for the case
                                                                  (i) two different
moments are identifable: one due to the thrust line offset from the aircraft
                                                                             cg and
the other due to the propeller nonnal force and its relative offset to the
                                                                                  cg.
Regarding slipstream sources (ii) they are due to the additional lift caused by
changes in dynamic pressure and downwash as described for the lift applied for
both the wing and tail. Otherwise there is also the natural
                                                              pitch moment of the




                                        127
propeller due to the asynmetry caused by wing circulation ow over the propeller
disc, however it is a very small.


(3) Drag: - Power effects on drag are due to (i) the change in slipstream dynamic
pressure on the prole drag of those portions of the aircraft immersed in the
propeller slipstream and (ii) the change in the induced drag due to the lift
component of the direct propeller forces (iii) and the change in angle of incidence
of the immersed portions of the wing due to the propeller downwash.


The direct effects, i.e., those originating 'om the propeller forces are more
accentuated at high power conditions. The effects due to the propeller slipstream
and downwash are more signicant at low speed and high power.


The Jetstream aerodynamic conguration is such that the thrust line is
                                                                          very close
to the cg position reducing the pitch moment caused by the thnst vector.
Otherwise the propeller installation is distant from the wing
                                                                     increasing the
moment due to the propeller normal force. The nacelle is very thin and the
propeller is installed almost following the wing chord resulting in maximum wing
immersed area and minimum nacelle contribution to lift, drag, etc. Thus liï        ¬
contribution due to the increment in dynamic pressure and downwash
                                                                            may be
signicant, however low speed phugoid involves low power condition. With
reference to the horizontal stabilizer it is installed almost out of the inuence of
the propeller slipstream main section minimizing the effects due to the increment
of dynamic pressure at the tail. For the phugoid case the incidence
                                                                    angle is not
signicant and incidence changes are very small such that the effects are
lTl1l'l1l'l'll.1I'l'l.


When dealing with aerodynamic derivatives detennined from
                                                            ight data, all the
effects described above are present in the ight data since it reects what is
happening to the aircra in the real world. In general all these terms can be
quantied, at least theoretically, by techniques such as those presented by
Wolowicz and Yancey [55]. When using a two step identication
                                                                    process, as the
one being used in the present research, the objective of the rst
                                                                 step is to identify
in the best way possible the aerodynamic tenns. For the Jetstream
                                                                       phugoid the
rst step consists in the identication of the force
                                                     components X and Z and the
moment M and other aircraft states as well. From the moment M are removed the
contributions due to the propeller thnst and propeller nonnal force
                                                                     resulting only
in the net aerodynamic component (if the
                                               propeller direct pitch moment is
considered small). Analogously the lift contribution due to the
                                                                 propeller nonnal
force is removed from the nonnal force Z
                                                  Aerodynamic models are then
fonnulated in the second step for the three components X Z and
                                                                      M with and
without the propeller force and moment
                                             components, by using the stepwise




                                        128
  regression method generating then the aerodynamic derivatives as coefcients of
  the models.



  6.6.2 Thrust and thrust excursion

 with different speed disturbances. Inputs
                                             causing speed disturbances of 5 and 10
 knots were perfonned resulting in
                                        phugoids with different speed excursion
 around the same common reference
                                         airspeed. This leads to different thrust
 excursions around the same reference speed.
                                                   Figure 6.18 below presents the
 propeller thrust of two different phugoids, at the same reference speed (120
 Kias), however with different speed excursion and consequently different thnst

                                                                              130
The mechanism of the thrust Variation is such that when the aircraft is in the
phugoid ascendent path the power is increasing. During this phase the moments
due to the thrust and the normal forces are positive increasing the pitch attitude.
At the same time the increased thmst helps to increase the peak in height and
avoids further reductions in speed. In the descending phase the power is reduced
and in some instances the propeller would even produce drag (negative torque).
I this phase the reduced moments contribute to reduce the pitch attitude while
the reduced thrust (or drag) reduces the speed and increases the height Variation.
In terms of speed the thrust Variation produces a more at phugoid.  It terms of
height excursion the resulting phugoid has its upper and lower segments
defonned (sharpened). Certainly the differences between the traveled distances
in the upper and lower segments are small than that observed in a phugoid of an
aircra with ordinary power control system.     Othewise it seems that the power
excursion increases the pitch excursion. This simple analysis does not allow to do
any conclusion regarding damping changes.

Thrust calculation was a serious problem during the campaign. I principle it was
decided to calculate thmst from the propeller charts using engine
                                                                     power
measurements from recorded engine torque. Aer initial tests it was observed
that the torque recorded values were completely unreliable. They had excess of
noise and unreal measurements.     Thrust figures used during the campaign were
those calculated directly by the Data Recording System which are based on charts
of engine parametric     power and propeller parametric thrust supplied by
aircra/engine manufacturers and presented in Appendix B.       The propeller
nomal force has been calculated by the procedure dened in NASA Technical
Note T D-6800 [55] and a typical graph of the propeller nonnal force
                                                                     during a
phugoid is presented in Appendix B.



6.6.3 State Estimation


State estimation in the sense of detennination of exact values for the aircraï
                                                                            ¬
states was performed by using an EKF, as presented in section 3,
                                                                applied to the
low speed phugoid manoeuvres. All data presented in section 6.6 was
                                                                    processed
with 25 samples per second.


Several phugoids were performed during the test
                                                     campaign. First tests were
devoted to dene an adequate input technique as described in
                                                                   chapter 5. The
input there denominated as '31 1' proved to be adequate to excite both longitudinal
modes. Figure 6.20 below presents a typical '31 1' input recorded
                                                                    during the test
campaign.

Some of the bias terms did not present the full characteristics of convergence as
expected for well estimated states. Therefore the accelerations corrected by their
nal bias values over all samples satised the six-degree-of-freedom equations of
motion denoting that they represent actual corrections to the measurements.
What in fact has been observed is that the phugoid is not a good manoeuvre to
identify such errors. Certainly bigger excursions in nonnal acceleration, pitch
rate and incidence would be required to improve the results. I other words, a
special manoeuvre should be perfonned before the phugoid with the purpose of
determining the bias tenns. A good practice would be to replace the incidence
by a calibrated incidence reducing in this way the identiability problem. The
same is valid for the airspeed. The measured rate of pitch presented a
                                                                       very small
bias (insignicant) while no attempts have been made to determine bias to the
lateral   acceleration,   rate   of roll   and    roll   attitude   measurements   due   to
identiability/recontructibility problems.
Final estimated states and corrected acceleration measurements where validated
through simulation when the estimated data reproduced acceptably the phugoid.

With reference to state estimation nal accuracy, the nal covariance matrix does
not presented any evidence of correlation among terms. An
                                                             analysis of the
individual covariance tems revealed good convergence and no sign of
                                                                    problems
during the estimation process.The analysis of the lter residual carried out
during lter tuning revealed no problem of mismodelling and the residuals
presented the required whiteness characteristics discussed in chapter 3.
Regarding the smoother lag, the lter time constant resulted in an average value
of 6, such that a smoother lag of 15 time intervals
                                                    (samples) was used, as for the
Tucano data.



6.6.4 Aerodynamic Models


(i) Excluding Power Eects.
Once the states have been estimated the next step was the detennination of the
aerodynamic models which were accomplished by the Stepwise Regression
Procedure. To do so the single states or its powers or derivatives were
                                                                        grouped in
les together with the independent variables (X, Z and
                                                          M). On an average les
contained 2000 samples of 14 variables. Models were then estimated in batch
processing for each of the independent variables. Hundreds of models were then
calculated, starting by the linear ones and adding to them the non-linear terms
tiying to improve curve tting.

The best models were those that presented
                                        biggest correlation coeicient (R2)
and between them the best were the ones that resulted in
                                                         highest 'F statistic




                                            135
number.    The importance of 'F' in the regression may be seen on recursive
regressions where the models with bigger 'F' statistics converge fast to the nal
coeffcient values. Models with small 'F' number reaching nal coeicient
values at the nal stages of the regression only. The experience processing
models showed that adding independent variables to models may improve R2
,however, may decreases 'F too. In general the best models are those with biggest
correlation coeffcient, however with the minimum number of independent
variables. With reference to the partial correlation coefcient F it has been
observed that in good models its minimum value was around 50 for the less
signicant variables. Thus F =12 as defined by Klein [3] and presented in
section 4.6 is a good value to be used in the programs.


Tables 6.7, 6.8 and 6.9 below present the coefcients of the models obtained for
X Z and M without the effects of propeller forces and moments. They represent
dimensional aerodynamic derivatives detennined during a phugoid perfonned at
 120 Kias (cg 26% MAC) with a 5 k speed disturbance, taken as reference
                                                                        during
the analysis.

The analysis of the longitudinal equations of motion showed that for an aircraï
                                                                             ¬
like the J etstream, with no angle between the thrust line and the longitudinal axis,
the only term in the equations that is dependent of the thrust is
                                                                     X.   Cook [21]
shows that X is given by;



                         X = -1/fi-2c +;í                                 (6.6)
                               a/     o.spVs a/

In a rst approximation X may be assumed to be equal to
                                                       -2CD.                 This is
sometimes an acceptable simplifcation because in general the derivatives
                                                                                  %
and
      g are very close to zero.   For the present case study, low speed phugoid,

% is probably very small and should be considered zero. The thrust derivative
however is not so small as it could be seen from a quick analysis of
                                                                     Figure 6.18
that shows the thrust varying during the phugoid.
The thrust derivative with respect to the speed were evaluated for the
                                                                           phugoids
by two different approaches;


(1) by calculating the derivative of the thrust and the derivative of the airspeed
with respect to the time by using the Fixed Lag smoother
                                                             (Chapter 3) and then
calculating the ratio between the two derivatives to


obtain(2)
    by using the Stepwise     Regression procedure (Chapter 2) to represent the
thrust as a function of airspeed, ight path, etc.


This has been carried out by first correcting the thrust
                                                  by the altitude Variations
(following engine manufacturer power charts) and then calculating the
derivatives. Figure 6.27.a and 6.27.b below present the thrust derivatives
calculated using the Fixed Lag smoother for two phugoids at a common reference
speed of 120 kias (6000 f altitude): (a) for 5 k speed disturbance and (b) for 10
k speed disturbance.
