                                                                 142
The calculated derivatives are negative, that is the thrust is inversely proportional
to the speed Variation and they are very similar for both phugoids. Similar values
were observed for phugoids performed at 150 Kt.


Using the Stepwise Regression to estimate models representing the thrust as a
function of airspeed and ight path, coefcients ranging between -220 and -250
were obtained for the airspeed coefcient of the model depending on whether an
intercept was included or not to the model, respectively. These values represent
the thrust derivatives with respect to the speed and are very close to the
                                                                           averaged
values obtained 'om gures 6.27a and 6.27b. In summary a thrust derivative of
approximately -220 N /ms*     is present during the phugoid (120 Kias, 5000 to
10000 f altitude).
Analysing equation 6.6 it is easy to demonstrate that this derivative contributes
very signicantly to the increase of X because the term due to the derivative is
bigger than that due to the drag (-2CD).      It is well know that X govems the
phugoid damping so that the Variations of the thrust during the phugoid
contribute to increase the damping of the Jetstream phugoid. Since the derivative
tenn is bigger than the drag tenn then if the engine had a more constant
                                                                        power
during the phugoid the aircra probably would be unstable in tenns of phugoid.
The College of Aeronautics already performed phugoids at maximum
                                                                         speed
(maximum power) when the engine control has no more authority to change the
power and the results show undamped oscillations. Note that the phugoids
performed during the present research had very small damping. Any positive
ight path during the phugoid would transform it to undamped. Otherwise the
thrust derivative is nearly constant, the reason why thrust Variations are not
observed in the X plottings.


Although the mechanism of the thrust Variation suggests that it may affect the

phugoid frequency, om the analysis of Z there has been no possibility to
validate this observation. From the test itself sometimes it
                                                             appears to be true. It
was observed in two pairs of phugoids with similar speed disturbances
                                                                         (5 and 10
kt) that the period of the phugoid with small speed disturbance was 2 seconds
longer. Anyvvay two seconds is also considered from previous tests as a natural
dispersion of the phugoid period.

It is important to note that Z was probably the less accurate of the calculated
normalised forces X Z and moment M        This is because Z depends on the bias
tems of the normal acceleration and pitch attitude. Any
                                                           inaccuracy in the bias
tenns cause small differences of phase in the resulting
                                                           specic force causing
changes in the acceleration W. As the phugoid damping is very small these small
differences cause divergences in the simulations. The bias terms had to be
adjusted by trial and error in a simulation in order to bring their values to the



                                        143
required accuracy to reproduce the actual phugoids. Final values accepted as the
average corrected ones were 0.034, 0.23 and -0.12 for the pitch attitude,
longitudinal and normal accelerations, respectively.   They have very small
differences to the values presented in gures 6.21, 6.22 and 6.23 respectively.
Otherwise, the bias terms of the pitch attitude, longitudinal and nonnal
accelerations seems to be variable. Analysing its fonnat it is identiable the
phugoid shape. These characteristics in bias terms are nonnally considered as
lack of adequate lter tuning. It is important to note that hundreds of attempts
have been carried out to improve these results, however without success at a cost
of 30 minutes processing time for every attempt. It seems sometimes that the IRS
is installed in a structure that is defonning during the manoeuvre so that the
signal shape is due to movement of the equipment. The Inertial Reference System
is xed to the seat rails.


The linear regression approach is not a accurate method for aircra parameter
estimation.   That is the reasons why it has been replaced by more powerful
techniques as the EKF and the Maximum Likelihood. However it is an extremely
exible technique to be used in non-linear conditions such as stall, spin, i.e., high
disturbances and high non-linearity. It was extremely easy to obtain hundreds of
non-linear models for the phugoids with a minimum time demand.
It has been implemented along the campaign an EKF to identify directly the
phugoids and to have comparative gures to validate the regression results.
Twice the EKF dynamic models were modied, however at the end of the
campaign the EKF was not updated to the nal model, so that no data is available
to compare with the regression models. The EKF lacks exibility.


Unfortunately, the phugoid controls 'ee is an oscillation with very small damping
requiring high accuracy mainly when analysing particularities such as the
Variation of power of the case study. Thus not favorable to the linear
                                                                       regression.
Otherwise, the recursive regression allowed to analyse the derivatives during the
manoueuvre which was an interesting feature of the linear regression.


It has been observed that the simple phugoid oscillation is nearly non identiable.
That is, if the measurements taken during the input are discarded then the models
obtained with the remaining measurements are not lly representative of the
actual oscillation.   The model's coefñcients vary point to point when processed
recursively or in batch with different segments of the oscillation (partitions of the
data). For instance processing the data backvvards the coefcients of the models
vary a lot then they begin to change drastically and start to converge when the
measurements taken during the input start to be processed. If the aircraft enters
into a phugoid oscillation without a control input then several
                                                                   aerodynamic
derivatives will not be identiable because some aircrañ states will not be well




                                         144
excited (nearly observable only). These are the case of the q and w derivatives for
instance which are well identiable only when the oscillation includes the short
period. In particular the M model will be poorly dened because it is highly
dependent of q. This has been observed above. In principle the X model will be
better dened because the excursions in speed and longitudinal acceleration are
large.


Summarizing, the linear regression was not accurate enough to identify changes
due to the thrust effects over signicant derivatives as X,Z and MW. The
changes in thrust due to different speed excursion were not too small (up to 250
lb), however were not identiable mainly regarding effects of slipstream and
downwash. It was not possible to increase the speed excursion due to the
extrapolation of thrust calculation in the low speed side of the propeller charts.
The required power to y at 120 kias was around 30% of the maximum power. A
low value to result in appreciable slipstream/downwash effects that are signicant
at high power only. Othewvise the phugoid is an oscillation at nearly constant
incidence. For the analysed phugoids the incidence values were around 4
degrees, therefore a small value holding the tail out of the main effects of
propeller. It has been considered the possibility to include test points with
undercarriage down and aps down in order to increase the power to a more
signicant value. However under these conditions the increase in drag increases
the damping reducing the possibility of the linear regression to detect the desired
phenomenon. Anyway, the increase in power for these cases would be of 20 to
30% only, a number still too low. It has been considered that the aircraft
                                                                                 cg
position does not affect signicantly the shape of the thrust derivative. A good
option would be to analyse phugoids at 130 Kt because in this case it is possible
to use speed disturbance of 15 Kt (instead of 10 Kt) increasing the difference in
thrust Variation with reference to the phugoid of 5 Kt speed disturbance.



6.6.5 The total least square.


The models presented in tables 6.7 to 6.9 were reprocessed using the Total Least
Square technique presented in chapter 4. Under this approach the errors in the so
called explanatory variables (independent variables) are taken into account in the
regression and not only in the dependent variable as in the ordinary Least Square
technique.
The Total Least Square solution was implemented by using equation 4.61. The
matrix Cu, tenns were dened as the standard deviations of the measurement
errors analogously to the measurement noise formulated for the Extended Kalman
Filter. With the exception of the terms in wz and u for which have been used
2wgo-    and 2ugo-, respectively, and placed in the matrix Cm, as cross-



                                       145
correlations terms, as dened by Laban [11]. For the intercept tenn and for the
diagonal terms of wz and u it was assumed a very small number (s= 1.0'1°), e.g.,



                                    o'          2 u o'â
                                         GW
                            Csd =                   8


                                                           O-77

for a model in u,w,w2,..., 77.


Each model (X, Z or M had its own matrix Cm, depending on the independent
variables composing the model.    The Total Least Square coefñcients calculated
for each model were not so different 'om those obtained using the ordinary Least
Square technique as would be expected. The standard deviation of the estimated
coefcients presented very similar values. It was expected different (bigger)
values for the TLS, however only the X model resulted in bigger deviations.
However, the models obtained 'om both techniques tted very well the estimated
forces and moments and it was hard to say which technique produced better
results. Tables 6.16, 6.17 and 6.18 below present the coefñcients and standard
deviations calculated by the two methods.

The Total Least Square calculations have been performed on a PC using Matlab.
The total samples used in the regressions have to be halved because both the
Matlab and the PC cannot handle matrices of size 2000 x 2000.                      The TLS
technique demands around 100 times the computing time required by the
traditional LS fonnulation, the main reason why it was not used extensively in the
research.



6.6.6 Propulsive efficiency


The determination of the propulsive efciency from ight data resulted in no
success. It was observed the same errors in the acceleration measurements as for
the   phugoids      however the    single     small   increment of power resulted             in



                                              147
manoeuvres not appropriate for the purpose of the bias tenns determination. No
acceptable convergence of the bias tems was obtained. The tests points were too
noisy due to high turbulence. It was very difficult to identify from measurement
traces what was measurement noise and what was the turbulence. Phugoids
performed in the same ight were inadequate for the phugoid analysis.
It was observed that due to the characteristics of the engine power control system
the actual power varies a lot during the aircra acceleration.    In principle the
process was developed for reciprocating engines which holds the power
invariable with the small speed increment. For the Jetstream case, however, aer
the power increment is applied the power increases but then it decreases
gradually as the speed increases teaching a nal equilibrium value for the new
stabilized speed (new incidence, new propeller torque demand). This
characteristic, however seems to not affect the use of the proposed technique.
Some altemative models have been used by assuming the power as an state
modelled as second order Gauss-Markov however no favourable results have
been achieved.


Some discrepancies between airspeed and acceleration measurements have been
observed. Measurement models containing only the longitudinal acceleration
were unable to reproduce the measured airspeed and accelerations derived from
the measured airspeed did not reproduce the measured acceleration.Several
different combinations of dynamic and measurement models have been tested
with no success.   It has not been identied whether the failure was due to the
EKF inability to solve the problem, improper fornulation of the models or
problems with the measurements. No attempts have been made to correct the bias
terms by simulation and trial and error as performed for the phugoid analysis.




                                       148
                                       Chapter 7



                                   CONCLUSIONS



7.1 Comparative Analysis of the Two Smoothers


A general analysis of the use of the two step Estimation-Before-Modelling
technique for aircra parameter estimation has been presented in the preceding
chapters. It has been shown that the Modied Bryson-Frazier (MBF) smoother,
the smoother most commonly used in the technique, produces very good results,
however it demands too much processing time and computer temporary data
storage. As some state derivatives are independent or even dependent variables
in aerodynamic models structure determination, that is, in the second step of the
identication procedure, then a differentiation process is nonnally required.   To
cope with these two requirements, i.e., to smooth and to differentiate, the Fixed
Lag (FL) smoother-differentiator has been proposed as a altemative to the MBF _
It has been observed during the research that;


(i)     Although much simpler than the MBF counterpart, the Fixed Lag
        smoother was found to work well in all the aircra applications tried
        so far. It would appear that the Fixed Lag smoother algorithm is capable
        of producing state estimates of equivalent accuracy to the MBF
        smoother in typical aircra parameter application, as shown in
        gures 6.12 to 6.14.


(ii)    The Fixed Lag smoother is robust. It has been used not only with the
        Tucano dutch roll data but also with the Jetstream phugoid, thrust and
        short period   data. It never diverged     while the MBF diverged as
        presented in section 6.4.


(iii)   The differentiation capability of the Fixed Lag smoother was found
        to be excellent, as shown in gures 6.15 and 6.16, and
                                                                 during the
        research it has been used even associated with the MBF smoother.


(iv)    Both smoother algorithms gave results of comparable accuracy,
        although the combination of the EKF-MBF returned             better
        correlation and covariance parameters than the IEKF-FL combination.
        However, it was found that by selectively tuning the time constants of
        the Fixed Lag smoother for each state variable the statistical match
        between the two methods may be improved. Improvements
                                                                    may also




                                        149
       be achieved by independently tuning the Kalman lters of both
       algorithms, as discussed in section 6.5.


(V)    The Fixed Lag smoother is computationally more efcient than the
       MBF smoother because it is faster and requires only a fraction of the
       temporary data storage required by the MBF (section 6.5).


(Vi)   The EKF-MBF algorithm determines bias terms that are at for the
       sampling interval while the IEKF-FL produces tenns that converge
       to a nal value similar to that estimated by the EKF-MBF, as shown
       for the EKF and MBF in Figure 6.9. VVhen using the Fixed
                                                                       Lag
       smoother the data has to be corrected by the nal values of the bias
       tenns and reprocessed in a simplied model without bias terms.
       Section 6.5.


(vn)   The Fixed Lag smoother is sensitive to the noise used in the Gauss-
       Markov modelled tenns. The biggest differences observed between
       the two algorithm were just in these tenns. It is required to
                                                                     supply
       enough noise to the three terms of each model in order to minimize
       the differences between algoritlnn results. This aspect is not
       signicant for terms like X Z and Y which are included in the
       measurement models nor to M that appears in the expression of q
       only. N and L are both included in the expressions of p and r and in
       this case it is not difficult for the lter to estimate different
                                                                         pairs
       of N and L resulting in equal values for the pair
                                                                  p,r by simply
       dening conveniently the amount of noise in N and L (section 6.5).


 v)    It has been observed that the lter time constant used to dene the
       Fixed Lag smoother lag resulted      in a value ranging between
       of 3 to 9 denoting than on average lag of 15
                                                      sampling intervals is
       is quite an acceptable value for general use. However, it
                                                                        may
       depend of the signal noise characteristics (sections 6.5 and 6.6.3).



7.2 Phugoid Analysis.


Four phugoids have been analysed using the E-B-M
                                                technique dened in terms of
the Fixed Lag smoother and the linear regression. The main
                                                            ndings of the
analysis were;


(i)    The Jetstream power Variation      with the airspeed and      ight path
       produces a thrust Variation that is inversely proportional to the speed.




                                         150
       I other words the derivative of the thrust with respect to the airspeed
       is negative. This derivative is an important element in the fonnation
       of X, the derivative that denes the phugoid damping characteristics.
       A large negative value of X resulting in a well damped phugoid while
       a positive X results in a divergent phugoid. Nonnally the dominant
       tenn in the denition of X is -2 CD. However, for the Jetstream

       the dominant term is
                             g which is around twice the value of -2CD.
       Thus the power Variation during the phugoid contributes positively to
       its damping. The thrust Variations during the phugoid are not shown

       directly in the X plotting because
                                              g is nearly constant. Figures
       6.27 a and b.


11)    Analysing separately by linear regression the force Z and moment M
       with and without the direct contribution of the propeller forces, the
       resulting aerodynamic derivatives show clearly the reduction in the
       aircra static margin due to the action of propeller forces (tables
       6.8, 6.9, 6.14 and 6.15).


111)   From the direct analysis of the ight data it seems that the period of
       the phugoids perfonned with small speed disturbances are bigger
       than the period of the phugoids performed with bigger speed
       disturbances. Thus it seems that the thrust Variation affects the
       phugoid frequency too. However, this nding has not been
       conrmed by the analysis of the aerodynamic derivatives because
       the dispersion observed on the determination of the Z derivatives
       were too large to be conclusive. Otherwise there are no thnst
       terms in the derivatives that govem the frequency of the phugoid
       (mainly Z) so that no conclusions can be made (section 6.6.4).


v)     Simple controls free phugoid oscillation (without input) are nearly
       non identiable by the linear regression process. A disturbance is
       required exciting also the short period in order to have a better
       identification the aerodynamic tenns (section 6.6.4).


V)     The linear regression is not accurate enough (or sensitive) to
                                                                      identify
       small effects like those due to the propeller slipstream and
       downwash over the immersed parts of the aircraft for the power level
       attained in the low speed phugoids (section 6.6.4).




                                        151
(vi)   The overall accuracy of the state estimation phase could be improved
       signicantly if, before the phugoids, had been perfonned manoeuvres
       conveniently designed to estimate the bias terms of the measurement
       model. In this case, when beginning the phugoid the lter would have
       the bias terms fully stabilised. The manoeuvre for example might
       consist of an excursion in lateral acceleration followed by an
       excursion in nonnal acceleration (incidence).However, for the
       Jetstream the Flight Data Recording System cannot cope with the
       with the recording of a too long manoeuvre.



7.3 The Total Least Square


The Total Least Square approach to linear regression presented results very
similar to those obtained with the ordinary Least Square technique, as shown in
tables 6.16 to 6.18. I tenns of calculated derivatives it is difficult to say which
technique produced the best results because there were no comparative terms
(e.g., results from other accurate technique). In tenns of covariance of the
estimates, the results were also very similar which in principle was not expected.
Maybe the formulation of the Matrix CM in tenns of the measurement noise was
not adequate to be used in the technique. However, it seems to be the best
information available on the accuracy of the variables.  Another important point
was the definition of the errors of the squared terms (u2,w3) which possibly
require further analysis, perhaps in follow on research.

      Linear Least Square Solution by Householder Transformation.



Let A(m, n) be a real matrix with m 2 n and rank n and z a vector. The objective
is to determine the vector â that minimizes the cost function,


                                 J(x) = Hz-Ax||2                        (A. 1)

Let   T(m,n) be an orthogonal matrix.           Due to the property              of the
orthogonal transformations, the cost function can be rewritten as,


                       J(x) = ||T(z- Ax)||2 = ||(Tz) - (TA)x||2         (A2)

The minimum of J (x) is independent of T, and T can be chosen so that it has a
computationally attractive form, TA be upper triangular, for example;

Equatiors and is less suceptible to errors due to computer roundof as stated by
Biennan [39].

                                    APPENDIX C



             U-D Covariance Factorization Update Data Processing.


Problems that involve frequent estimates are more effciently handled using
Kalman's sequential covariance estimation rather than the ordinary formulation,
Bierman [39]. Othewvise the use of triangular matrices involves fewer arithmetic
operations, reduces computer storage requirements, may eliminate the calculation
of square roots and may even eliminate the need for matrix inversion. This is the
case of the covariance update when dening the covariance matrix in the fonnat;


                                           P = UDUT                                C.1

with U upper triangular and D diagonal, i.e., the U-D factorized covariance.


The update of U and D can be easily accomplished by the following the
procedure formulated in Bierman [39] as Theorem V.3.l. Suppose that a a
priori estimate of xK_1 and covariance PK_1, dened as C.l, is to be combined
with a scalar measurement z given by,



                                             ,               E(w)=0
                      z=H,TKxK+w           wlth
                                                         {E(w2)=r                  C2

The Kalman gain KK and the updated covariance factors
                                                      UK and DK are
calculated by;


                         f=U7Ã-1H      ,     fT=(f---ºf)                           C-3
                          v=DK_1f      ,         v,.=d,.K_f,.     '=l,...,n        C.4


                   d1K=d1K_r/al ,    a1=r+vlf1           ,      K2T=(v1,0,...,0)   C.5

For j = 2,...,n recursively cycle the following equations;


                                  aJ.=a]._l+v1.fJ.                                 C_6
                                d.JK =d. a. /a.                                    C7
                                        JK- J1 J
                       "JK = uff., +ÄfKf º       '11 = `ff /af-1                   C-8
                                                                                   C.9

                                         KK=K/a                      C.11

                                    APPENDIX D



                                  Ridge Regression



Let A be the m x n matrix of measured independent variables and Y the m x 1
vector of measured dependent variables with mzn. Let A be the matrix A in
correlation form, that is centered and scaled as dened in Draper and Smith [36].
The Ridge Regression of Y against A is calculated by;


                              ®(<) = [AfA + K1]-IAZY                        (D. 1)

where c is a positive number that normaly lies in the range (0,1).


Note that Y is not in correlation fonn and ®(c) is a vector with no
                                                                    intercept
estimates.
When c= 0 then (š)(0) is the usual solution to the least square problem,


                                 ®(0) = [AfA]`1AfY                          (D-2)

As the matrix A is in correlation form then the regression coefcients
                                                                              ®(k)J. must
be reparametrized by calculating,


                                    _®</«º-       .=
                                                 0


                              1,2,...,n)eo
                               = Y-                                         (D4)


                                S1, = pfj., _ fly                           (D5)
                                      `=l

Here Y and xj denotes averages and (~) is the intercept.


The choice of the value of K is carried out by
                                                       plotting the coefcients (`~)( c)1.â
against K. The analysis of the plots reveals that;


(i) For a certain value of K. the coefcients stabilize, that is, tend to be parallel
to the x-axis, showing the characteristcs of an
                                                orthogonal system.

(ii) The ill-conditioned coefcients at<= 0 will loss the large absolute value
    converging to its true value as K changes from zero..



                                         166
(iii) Coefcients with apparently incorrect signs at <= 0 will have changed to
      their proper signs.


The nal coefcients are not least square estimates and are biased, however
                                                                      they
provide a smaller overall mean square error because the achieved variance
reduction will compensate for the introduced bias.
